# Canonical Decision Record — Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey

**October Term 1991 • Nos. 91-744 and 91-902 • Merits decision and cross-review • June 29, 1992 • Version 1.2 correction**

## Event, correction authority and chronology

This record corrects and supersedes the operative Casey adjudication in [the June 29, 1992 version 1 record](Planned_Parenthood_of_Southeastern_Pennsylvania_v_Casey_merits_1992-06-29.md). The earlier record remains unmodified as the superseded version. The user, as author of the Engine, expressly authorized reopening this finally adjudicated event on September 15, 2026. That direction displaces the default against retroactive revision for this matter only. Version 1.1 was superseded before any adjudication under it was committed and produced no Decision Record; its drafting choices are not attributed to Stone as prior judicial positions.

The corrected event retains its effective date of **June 29, 1992**, argument date of **April 22, 1992**, and dockets. The established June 29 sequence is **Casey, Lucas, Espinosa**, following the term inventory. Those uncoordinated same-day cases share the entering-law baseline; presentation order creates no otherwise unsupported dependency. Commitment of this correction is a later ledger entry, not a new in-world decision date. The overall chronology cursor remains **July 17, 1992, after Benten v. Kessler**, subject to the completed dependency review below.

Five clinics and a physician sought declaratory and injunctive relief before enforcement of specified 1988 and 1989 amendments to Pennsylvania's Abortion Control Act. Review lies under **28 U.S.C. §1254(1)** from the Third Circuit's October 21, 1991 judgment, **947 F.2d 682**, reviewing **744 F. Supp. 1323 (E.D. Pa. 1990)**. The district court held a trial in the week of July 30, 1990. It invalidated the emergency definition and connected restrictions, prescribed informed consent, parental informed consent, spousal notice, selected confidential-report items, and public release of facility reports. It sustained other reporting and gestational-age ascertainment requirements; the providers did not appeal those adverse rulings. The Third Circuit sustained the appealed provisions except spousal notice. Providers seek broader relief in No. 91-744; officials seek restoration of spousal notice in No. 91-902. January 21, 1992 grants are verified source chronology, not newly invented separate simulated grant events.

The questions concern constitutional protection of the abortion decision and reconsideration of Roe; medical emergency; medical and ancillary disclosures, optional materials, delivery, waiting and certification; parental informed consent and judicial bypass; spousal notification; and the appealed reporting and public-release provisions. The briefing separately presents professional-speech objections and spousal-notice equality and marital/informational-privacy objections. A conditional request to remand alternative constitutional foundations if Roe is abandoned is disputed as to preservation. Neither constitutional fetal personhood, a general funding entitlement, the unchallenged sex-selection prohibition, every postviability restriction, nor district-upheld unappealed reporting is converted into a fresh merits question.

## Participation and law entering the event

Chief Justice Alex-Lamar Stone-Zsela and Justices White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas participate at both argument and decision. Thomas's October 23, 1991 oath predates argument. No recusal or nonparticipation is established. Nine participate; six constitute a quorum and five votes control a judgment component. Assignment authority is component-specific: the Chief when he belongs to its judgment coalition; otherwise the senior participating Associate in that coalition, in the order White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas. Assignment does not determine authorship or reasoning joins.

The opening sources are `state/HOLDINGS.md`, `state/STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` and `state/STANDING_STATE.md`, edition **September 14, 2026**, last completed simulated term **None**, processed through the **October 7, 1991 term-opening baseline**. The first two contain no simulated rules; there is consequently no abortion entry with a `Present operation effective` or `Current wording authority` field to quote. Surviving pre-divergence law remains law. Earlier validated OT1991 decisions supplement the opening baseline only within their actual scope. No earlier effective simulated abortion replacement enters this event; the superseded Casey version 1 cannot supply its own entering law.

### Issue-specific entering-law snapshots

- **Protected choice, implementation and precedent.** Roe and Doe recognize constitutional protection for the decision, Roe's received trimester and viability structure, and the life-and-health safeguard. Akron and Thornburgh remain controlling within their actual holdings unless this decision changes them. Webster, Hodgson and Ohio v. Akron Center must be read by proposition and joinder; their fractures do not already establish a general replacement abortion framework. Marks supplies the date-eligible narrowest-grounds inquiry, subject to the Engine's conservative logical-subset check. The eight Justices' prior separate theories are evidence for modeling, not existing Court law.
- **Emergency.** The statute's good-faith clinical judgment, immediate-death branch and prospective serious-risk/major-function branch are distinct from the Third Circuit's construction covering the three disputed conditions at onset. Doe and Thornburgh supply health-protection premises. The separate §3205(c) defense for omitted information does not authorize delay of necessary treatment. The adopted construction, its limits and available relief must be identified rather than treating a federal construction as Pennsylvania statutory words.
- **Information, speech, delivery and delay.** Danforth allows genuine informed consent. Akron and Thornburgh constrain compulsory persuasion, arbitrary delay and unjustified interference with information delivery and medical judgment. Riley, Wooley, Barnette and Zauderer frame the separately preserved speech question; compensation alone does not decide its classification. Medical content, its speaker, ancillary notices, voluntary receipt of materials, timing, and certification are separate components. Statutory rape permission and the disclosure-health defense must be applied in their own terms.
- **Minors and bypass.** Danforth, Bellotti, Ashcroft, Hodgson and Ohio v. Akron Center permit specified parental involvement with constitutionally adequate alternatives; minors' welfare and parental roles differ from the relationship between adult spouses. Maturity and informed consent, or best interests, are alternative authorization grounds. The district's parental-attendance finding and the Third Circuit's reservation are not statutory visit commands. The internally adequate bypass and its claimed ability to cure an added burden on a willing-parent case are distinct issues.
- **Spousal notice.** Danforth denies a spouse a veto over the protected abortion choice. Eisenstadt treats privacy as the individual's right, including within marriage. The record supplies practical coercion and the exceptions' limits; it does not make all marriages abusive. Preserved equality and privacy arguments remain separable from the abortion-liberty ground. The governing population and facial-relief standard must be stated at the level actually adopted, without importing later law.
- **Reporting, public release and remedy.** Danforth permits appropriately directed confidential medical reporting; Thornburgh requires attention to burdens and disclosure consequences. Adequacy of confidentiality is not disputed here. The referral identity, three medical-judgment predicates, spousal fields and two public-release requirements must be judged separately; unappealed ordinary reporting and unchallenged facility-report collection remain outside fresh merits reach. Brockett and the supplied state severability clause support only legally available, independently operative severance, not wholesale judicial reconstruction.
- **Earlier current-term continuity.** Foucha protects against psychiatric custody after its substantive predicate ends; Riggins requires a justified override of an established bodily-refusal interest. Neither assigns an abortion burden. Medina expressly leaves different procedural methods fractured and preserves particular competence-hearing safeguards. Lee distinguishes practical pressure from formal permission in its own setting. Hilton and Quill demonstrate proposition-specific precedent treatment and particular reliance, with important statutory or congressional-adjustment differences. The appended frozen packet and audit cite each Justice's actual published position, including Thomas's qualified adherence in McCollum and Quill. No borrowed label decides this case.

## Neutral preflight, statutory source discipline and mode

The corrected neutral packet, its version 1.2 source clarifications and the user's statutory-only appendix were reopened anew. Both lower-court opinions, including all findings, footnotes and separate material, were read in full from Caselaw Access Project texts. The providers' opening and reply, officials' response, Solicitor General's brief, providers' supplemental with its appendix, and official April 22 argument were read in full. The five IA brief PDFs were verified against current item metadata and freshly extracted. The source map below identifies each reader's completed checks and locations; no unperformed check is described as passed.

The complete statutory quotations and the neutral distinction among text, findings, lower-court construction and advocacy are preserved in the appended frozen Neutral Modeling Packet. The paternal-support qualification is a **permission**: **“In the case of rape, this information may be omitted.”** (§3205(a)(2)(iii), supplied statutory source.) It has no police-reporting condition and does not erase a support obligation. It is distinct from §3209(b)(3), which requires spousal sexual assault resulting in the pregnancy and a report to law enforcement with jurisdiction, with the incorporated 90-day restriction described in district finding 297. The supplied appendix's penalty label “(e)” is an OCR artifact; the lower courts identify the quoted paragraph as §3205(c). The source file is preserved as supplied, and the correction is labeled rather than silently made to it.

**Single-conversation-mode disclosure.** True blindness was unavailable. The user's prompt exposed a summary of the revised position before file-stage work; source retrieval also incidentally returned historical Casey snippets. Those exposures are disclosed in the control log without supplying them as modeling premises. Fresh-context creation failed with `agent thread limit reached`, including a separate child-agent attempt. The Engine's single-conversation fallback was therefore used with scoped source handoffs, a separately validated neutral packet, new provisional commitments, a frozen comparator handoff, and the full approved Stone text opened last. No version 1 or version 1.1 commitment is inherited. The audit records decisional conclusions and their legal support, not fictional dialogue, draft history or hidden reasoning traces.


## What this correction changes

**Source corrections and clarified scope.** The statute always permitted, rather than required, omission of paternal-support information in rape, without a police-reporting condition (§3205(a)(2)(iii)). The separate disclosure defense requires preponderance proof of a reasonable belief that furnishing information would severely adversely affect physical or mental health (§3205(c)); it does not waive waiting. Optional receipt of materials is different from mandatory notice of their availability (§3205(a)(2)–(3)); district findings 181–189 do not establish that every pamphlet is false. Parental attendance was a district operational finding, while the Third Circuit reserved statutory construction in footnote 23; neither an express visit command nor incorporation of every adult delivery/timing mechanism may be invented. The public-funding lookbacks differ between §3207(b), measured before an inspection request, and §3214(f), measured before filing. Reporting fields, unappealed requirements, preservation and ordinary remand are now separated. Full briefs and argument replace the old record's expressly incomplete brief reading. These corrections concern source description and scope, not amendments to Pennsylvania law or a claim that the historical Justices had different statutory text.

**Substantive changes in Stone's approved position from committed version 1.** Stone now affirmatively proposes a six-step undue-burden framework replacing rigid trimester machinery while retaining Roe's constitutional foundation. It independently prohibits a substantial obstacle to protected previability care and also tests a smaller meaningful burden against State-substantiated benefit, fit and practicable alternatives. He qualifies categorical treatment of neutral ancillary disclosures in Akron and Thornburgh and the no-valid-applications formulation in Ohio v. Akron Center. He sustains concise accurate ancillary notices, seeks focused proceedings and interim protection concerning particular materials and parental attendance, and opposes automatic public release and blanket referrer identification. He continues to oppose unjustified physician exclusivity and fixed delay, preserves prospective emergency protection, ordinary medical information, a lawful parental/bypass structure and limited confidential medical-basis reports, and invalidates spousal notice and its dependent reports. His more explicit clinical-evidence, enforcement and remedial propositions are tested individually below. No Court authorship or result follows from his proposal alone.

**No version 1.1 judicial position.** Version 1.1 produced no committed adjudication. Version 1.2's two statements describing changes from v1.1 concern the user's drafting history: they do not establish a prior Stone vote, a prior Court holding, or a separate correction target. The only corrected committed matter is version 1, effective June 29, 1992. Each non-Stone position was remodeled anew; departures from version 1 and the historical comparator are separately recorded in the audit.

**Superseded version 1 law.** The earlier joint general formulation, its asserted issue-specific Marks treatment, its separate facial-vagueness holding, its five-vote generalized large-fraction formulation, its undifferentiated reporting permissions and whole notice-paragraph treatment have no continuing authority by virtue of that record. Only the propositions independently adopted and identified in this version 1.2 decision enter current law. Similar surviving outcomes rest on the new judgment and reasoning coalitions, never on an inherited version 1 commitment.

## Stone's fixed input and its treatment

The approved source is [version 1.2, approved September 15, 2026](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_V1_2.md). The complete A–E text was opened only after the global neutral commitments and comparator reconciliations were frozen. A mechanical comparison confirms its substantive A–E text, with headings promoted one level and line endings normalized for comparison, appears in both the combined chunk brief and runtime Stone split. The splices retain an additional version/status header; that does not change the substantive text. No source file was edited.

Stone's clinical-appropriateness limitation concerns safe and suitable care, not a requirement that a previability patient prove medical necessity or obtain official approval of her reasons. Professional consensus receives substantial evidentiary weight, not a veto. His meaningful-burden trigger is concrete, nontrivial interference with access, health, privacy or informed decision-making, not disagreement. Challengers establish the obligation, affected population, causation and meaningful burden, or independently establish prohibited purpose; the State then substantiates benefit and fit. No universal proof quantum is proposed. A demonstrated danger may justify necessary safety action; a safety label cannot remove otherwise appropriate care from protection. His protection of professionally defensible judgment is not immunity for fraud, malpractice or misconduct, nor an assertion that Colautti already announced comprehensive immunity.

The six steps and each qualification are tested in the assembly annex against every Justice's dated commitments. The operative judgment table records Stone's votes even where the Court rejects his requested remedy. No majority judgment is used to narrow or override his express position. The approved standing fallback is confined to separately named silent components in the final assembly table; it supplies no new ground and never substitutes for his explicit material-accuracy, clinical, scope or remedy reasoning.

**Each fallback use.** Stone joins the majority’s retention of the independently severable marital-status question in §3214(a)(12), adding no severance ground. He also joins the majority’s decision not to reach the distinct spousal First Amendment claim, the independent general marital/informational-privacy claim, and the standalone emergency-vagueness claim, including their unresolved preservation questions, without adding a ground. His equality nonreach is expressly stated in Section II A and is not a fallback use. His other stated provision judgments, information and clinical reasons, focused-remand requests and interim protections remain express positions and are never supplied or changed through fallback.


## Public action, judgment and remedy

The Court affirms in No. 91-902. In No. 91-744 it affirms in part, reverses in part and remands. It retains constitutional protection for previability choice, invalidates spousal notice and its notice-compliance reporting, preserves the independent confidential marital-status question, and sustains the remaining challenged requirements at the scopes and divisions below. It orders no focused materials or parental-attendance vacatur or interim injunction. There is no single omnibus vote for every aspect of the providers’ docket; the component table governs.

### Issues not reached and exact separate treatment

| Independent issue | Justice-specific treatment | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Standalone emergency vagueness and its preservation | All nine leave an independent merits/waiver determination unmade; the emergency judgment rests on the adopted protective construction | The superseded version 1 clarity holding is not current law. No waiver is invented. |
| Distinct spousal First Amendment theory and its preservation | All nine leave independent merits and preservation unresolved; the six notice-invalidating Justices obtain complete relief on liberty. White, Scalia and Thomas would decide only their adjudicated grounds and leave ordinary handling of any genuinely preserved live theory | No speech holding or finding of abandonment follows from the notice judgment. |
| Independent spousal equal protection | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter do not reach an independently sufficient ground; O’Connor would invalidate on that ground; White, Scalia and Thomas would reject the claim, with different tier-selection positions stated in topology | Five do not reach; one would invalidate; three would sustain. No majority equality merits rule. |
| Independent marital/informational privacy | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter do not reach an independently sufficient general ground; O’Connor would invalidate on a narrow adult reproductive-communication ground; White, Scalia and Thomas reject the particular independent claim | Five do not reach; one would invalidate; three would sustain. Autonomy and coercion remain part of the controlling abortion-liberty rationale. |
| Provider speech concerning accurate medical content and concise ancillary notices | All nine reject the narrow clinical-content claim. Eight reject the narrow ancillary claim; Blackmun does not reach that additional ground because his liberty view supplies relief | No all-professional-speech commercial classification, universal factual-speech permission, or decision that every particular pamphlet is accurate. |

| Adult-incapacity guardian class; unappealed ordinary reports and underlying gestational ascertainment; unchallenged sex-selection and other postviability provisions; funding entitlement; fetal personhood; conscience sources | Outside fresh merits reach at this event | Existing procedural status and applicable independent law remain. No drug-admission entitlement or unpresented Benten claim is created. |

Supreme Court merits review concludes in both dockets. In No. 91-902, the invalidation of §3209 is affirmed, 6–3. In No. 91-744, the judgment is affirmed in part and reversed in part, and the cause is remanded for proceedings consistent with the component holdings. The appellate approval of notice-given and nonnotice-reason reporting is reversed; those questions and their dependent enforcement must be removed, while the independently operating confidential marital-status question is retained under Act 64’s severability clause. The remaining challenged requirements are sustained at their stated scopes. No focused materials or attendance vacatur, separate remand, or interim injunction is ordered. The Court does not enjoin the sustained fixed waiting or physician-delivery requirements. The lower courts must conform relief and forms to the invalid notice obligations, preserve lawful independently operating requirements and respect the stated statutory defenses and culpability terms. No wholesale invalidation, new prosecution, damages award, expedited mandate, rehearing outcome or invented deadline is ordered. A later actual unlawful application remains challengeable through the appropriate proceedings; that reservation is not a newly retained Supreme Court merits matter.

## Opinion topology

The principal writings are Stevens’s opinion and O’Connor’s opinion, each speaking for the Court only in the parts identified below. O’Connor, Kennedy and Souter join every part of Stevens’s principal writing. White, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas join every part of O’Connor’s principal writing. All other joins are partial and are stated by issue. The dedicated governing-standard entry is a composite statement of the resulting law, not an additional unified opinion.

| Writing and exact part | Author | Direct joins, including author | Count and status | Relationship to judgment and limits |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| The framework retaining protected choice | Stevens | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter | 6; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Protected-choice foundation and retained boundaries |
| Stare decisis and the constitutional foundation | Stevens | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter | 6; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Ordinary precedent grounds for retaining the foundation |
| Medical emergency and timely clinical judgment | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Prospective statutory construction only; no independent vagueness holding |
| Accurate material medical information, including gestational age | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Accurate medical content, separated from its delivery and timing |
| Notice that free state information is available | Stevens | Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 8; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Concise factual availability notice |
| Notice of potentially available medical assistance | Stevens | Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 8; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Conditional assistance information |
| Paternal-support information and the rape omission permission | Stevens | Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 8; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Accurate support liability and the rape omission permission |
| Lawful publication and the patient’s choice to receive materials | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Lawful publication and genuinely elective receipt |
| The requested focused proceedings concerning particular materials | O'Connor | White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 8; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Present record does not warrant focused vacatur or interim suspension; no blanket accuracy finding |
| Accurate information supporting informed parental consent | Stevens | Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 8; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Lawful informed-parent content at the actual lower-court construction’s scope |
| One-parent informed consent and independent judicial authorization | Stevens | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter | 6; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Constitutionally necessary independent authorization safeguards; three additional judgment votes do not join necessity |
| The separate parental-attendance objection | O'Connor | White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 8; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Limited facial-record and construction ground; no universal curative-bypass rule |
| Spousal notice and State-enabled control of protected choice | Stevens | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter | 6; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Protected-choice prohibition of demonstrated practical spousal control |
| Confidential identification of referring physicians, agencies and services | O'Connor | White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 6; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Confidential clinical follow-up on the present record |
| Confidential basis for a medical-necessity judgment | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | After-care documentation of the precise necessity predicate |
| Confidential basis for an emergency judgment | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | After-care documentation without narrowing the prospective emergency |
| Confidential account of gestational-age determination | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Actual inquiry/test types and diagnostic basis; no new test or preapproval |
| Notice-compliance reporting and the severable marital-status field | Stevens | Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter | 5; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Complete notice-field invalidation and independent marital-status severance; Stone additionally joins the notice-invalidity proposition only |
| Written certification of lawful receipt | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Lawful receipt documentation only; ancillary and timing/delivery links carry their own coalitions |
| The independent speech objection to medical-consent information | Stevens | Stone, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 9; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Independent, narrow factual medical-consent First Amendment ground |
| The independent speech objection to concise ancillary notices | Stevens | Stone, White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 8; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Independent, narrow factual ancillary-notice First Amendment ground; Blackmun does not reach this additional ground |
| Physician delivery of the specified medical information | O'Connor | White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | 5; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Sufficient permission for this physician responsibility on this facial record |
| The fixed twenty-four-hour interval | O'Connor | White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | 5; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Sufficient permission for this deliberation interval on this facial record |
| Public access to facility and affiliation reports | O'Connor | White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 7; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Institutional public-funding accountability and the actual incremental-injury record |
| Public access to quarterly and trimester totals | O'Connor | White, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas | 7; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Aggregate public-funding accountability with the distinct filing lookback |
| The trimester timetable and the timing of regulation | O'Connor | Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | 6; Opinion of the Court at this scope | Operative timing change, severed from constitutional-foundation and complete-framework questions |
| Stone: proposed substantive framework, Steps 1–5 | Stone | Stone, Stevens | 2; noncontrolling at this scope | Full meaningful-burden/State-justification approach, including the independent obstacle bar and express clinical/causation limits |
| Stone: Step 6 generalized facial-relief qualification | Stone | Stone | 1; noncontrolling at this scope | Prohibited terms or large fraction of independently defined restriction cases; actual prevalence and insufficiency of narrower relief required; no numerical cutoff |
| Stone: clinical-evidence and enforcement protections | Stone | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Souter | 4; noncontrolling at this scope | Reliable evidence, substantial weight for consensus without veto, and protection of professionally defensible judgment in medical-interference inquiry; no universal State-proof transfer is attributed to Souter |
| Stone: delay and physician-exclusivity applications | Stone | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens | 3; noncontrolling at this scope | Inadequate justification under his stated approach; Blackmun joins the insufficiency as a minimum. Souter agrees with invalidation on his own Akron-adherence ground, without joining the complete new-framework application |
| Stone: blanket referral-identity objection | Stone | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens | 3; noncontrolling at this scope | Proved referral deterrence and inadequate incremental justification; no patient-publicity premise |
| Stone: automatic public-release objection | Stone | Stone, Blackmun | 2; noncontrolling at this scope | Actual disclosure burdens and inadequate fit compared with less exposing public-accounting alternatives |
| Stone: particular-materials and parental-attendance vacaturs, remands and interim injunctions | Stone | Stone | 1; noncontrolling at this scope | Focused proceedings and interim protection as stated in his approved disposition; waiting invalidation would remain effective |
| Blackmun: complete separate opinion | Blackmun | Blackmun | 1; noncontrolling at this scope | Stricter retained staged scrutiny; additional ancillary-content, referral and public-release objections, with limited medical-report permissions |
| Stevens: complete separate opinion | Stevens | Stevens | 1; noncontrolling at this scope | Meaningful justification and fit; refuses bare timetable erasure without operative protective replacement; differentiated information and reporting applications |
| Souter: complete separate opinion | Souter | Souter | 1; noncontrolling at this scope | Special justification for precise precedent changes; no complete replacement formula; Akron-based delivery/waiting dissent and narrow sustaining applications |
| O’Connor: separate severity-first framework and independent spousal grounds | O'Connor | O'Connor | 1; noncontrolling at this scope | Severity before compelling justification, rational review below; independently sufficient narrow sex-equality and adult reproductive-communication grounds |
| White: abortion foundation, stare decisis, spousal-notice and privacy analysis, report position and scope reservations | White | White, Scalia, Thomas | 3; noncontrolling at this scope | Abortion-specific rational-review position, no universal Salerno rule, no conditional report-severance vote; independent rights and preservation remain distinct |
| White: intermediate scrutiny is the governing sex-classification tier | White | White, O'Connor | 2; noncontrolling at this scope | Actual tier selection; Scalia and Thomas expressly assume without deciding, so do not join this selection |
| White: spousal equality application under intermediate scrutiny | White | White, Scalia, Thomas | 3; noncontrolling at this scope | Important parental-participation objective and asserted substantial fit; White applies the tier, Scalia and Thomas assume it |

The entire Stone six-step replacement has Stone alone; Stevens joins the substantive first five steps, with the separate clinical and application joins shown above. No joint opinion adopts a complete general substantial-obstacle replacement. White and O’Connor agree on selecting intermediate scrutiny for the spousal sex classification, but reject each other’s application; two tier-selection votes do not establish an independent equality holding. Scalia and Thomas assume that tier without deciding it. Souter’s delivery and waiting votes rest on his separate Akron-adherence ground and do not become joins in Stone’s full framework application.

The receipt-certification part has all nine only for ordinary lawful receipt documentation. The ancillary links have all except Blackmun; timing and delivery links have White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Stone joins the notice-invalidity proposition with the five complete signers of the notice-report part, but joins marital-status retention without its severance rationale. No White, Scalia or Thomas conditional vote for removing notice fields is counted.

## Controlling holdings

### The framework retaining protected choice

**Holding and operative rule.** The Fourteenth Amendment protects the decision to obtain an abortion before viability against prohibition and State-imposed transfer of effective control to another person. The State retains legitimate interests in health and potential life throughout pregnancy and may regulate within the surviving constitutional protections; after viability it may prohibit abortion subject to abortion necessary, in appropriate medical judgment, to preserve the woman's life or health.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “The framework retaining protected choice,” joined at this scope by Stone, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter: 6 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Protected-choice foundation and retained boundaries.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the Constitution continues to protect the decision whether to end a pregnancy before viability. That protection belongs to the person whose body, health and future the decision concerns. It does not depend on a legislature's willingness to approve her reasons. Before viability, the State may not convert its preference for childbirth into authority to prohibit the protected choice. Nor may it condition care on another person's effective control of that choice. This is a constitutional boundary, not a promise that every regulation affecting abortion is invalid. The separate holdings identify the regulations that this record permits and those that it forbids.

Roe v. Wade supplies the previability protection and the boundary associated with the fetus's capacity for life outside the woman. Doe v. Bolton protects the exercise of medical judgment and rejects unnecessary institutional approval as a condition of care. Together they distinguish a decision protected by liberty from an unrestricted license to practice medicine without standards. A competent patient gives voluntary informed consent, and a qualified clinician evaluates the safety and suitability of treatment. A previability patient need not establish that ending the pregnancy is medically necessary. Clinical appropriateness concerns the care provided, not official permission for her decision. The Court does not adjudicate every postviability restriction in reviewing these particular amendments.

The protection has a constitutional foundation beyond any single arrangement of pregnancy into stages. Griswold v. Connecticut recognizes protection for intimate reproductive decisions. Eisenstadt v. Baird makes clear that the individual does not lose decisional privacy by entering marriage. Carey v. Population Services International applies that protection to reproductive choice while allowing regulation to be evaluated in its actual setting. These authorities answer the contention that the absence of the word abortion from the constitutional text ends the inquiry. Liberty protects decisions concerning bodily integrity and family life even when the text does not list each protected activity. That reasoning does not dispense with identifying the particular protected interest or decide every claim described as personal autonomy.

The State's interest in potential life is real. It may express its preference, support childbirth and provide lawful information about assistance and alternatives. It may protect patients through appropriate professional standards, informed consent and medical regulation. Those interests exist throughout pregnancy. Their existence, however, does not answer whether a particular condition unlawfully interferes with the protected decision. The distinction matters here: accurate medical information and an effective parental alternative raise different questions from compulsory disclosure that gives an adult spouse practical power to prevent care. The Court therefore decides each requirement on its own terms, including its exceptions and actual operation.

Webster v. Reproductive Health Services did not produce a majority replacing Roe's general framework or withdrawing its core protection. The fragmented opinions there cannot be treated as an earlier overruling. This decision likewise identifies agreement by proposition. Six Justices retain the constitutional foundation and the viability and life-and-health boundaries. Their agreement does not make every proposed regulatory formula an opinion of the Court. The dedicated holding on the governing previability standard states the present force of the competing formulations and of earlier regulatory precedents. No fetal-personhood rule, entitlement to public funding, general immunity from medical law or entitlement to admission of a particular drug follows. Those questions are outside this judgment.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Roe v. Wade, 410 U.S. 113 — The constitutional foundation, previability choice, viability boundary and necessary life-and-health protection are retained; regulatory implementation is addressed separately.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — Protection of individualized clinical judgment remains; no new comprehensive immunity is adopted.
- Griswold v. Connecticut, 381 U.S. 479 — Applied for constitutional protection of intimate reproductive decisions.
- Eisenstadt v. Baird, 405 U.S. 438 — Applied to the individual's reproductive choice, including within marriage.
- Carey v. Population Services International, 431 U.S. 678 — Applied for protected reproductive decision-making without mechanically deciding every abortion regulation.
- Webster v. Reproductive Health Services, 492 U.S. 490 — Its fragmentation is recognized; no earlier majority overruling of Roe is inferred.

### The standard governing previability regulation after this decision

**Holding and operative rule.** Lower courts must apply the retained constitutional protection and the direct, provision-specific holdings of this decision, together with earlier rules not displaced here. Roe’s trimester timetable no longer determines when health and potential-life regulation may begin, but neither Stone’s complete six-step framework nor a general purpose-or-effect substantial-obstacle replacement commands a majority; absence of a substantial obstacle is therefore not a general safe harbor for every lesser burden.

**Authority.** Composite statement of the law of the decision, derived from the named opinion parts and their distinct coalitions below. It is not a separately signed unified framework opinion: protected choice and stare decisis have Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy and Souter; the timetable change has Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas; each provision carries its own express joins. The analysis identifies the force and limits of those actual holdings under Marks without adding a new majority formula.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court’s judgment changes some regulatory rules while retaining the constitutional protection of the decision. Lower courts must begin with the proposition actually at issue. Six Justices retain previability choice and the viability and necessary life-and-health boundaries. A different six reject Roe’s trimester timetable as the rule determining when health and potential-life regulation may begin. That negative holding permits pursuit of those interests throughout pregnancy, subject to independently applicable constitutional limitations. It does not supply the affirmative test for every regulation. The Justices who reject constitutional protection altogether are not counted as joining its retention; the Justices who preserve that protection are not all counted as joining the timetable change.

The direct provision holdings supply the next instruction. The present physician-delivery requirement and fixed waiting interval survive under an expressly limited five-Justice permission: the accepted costs and delays do not establish severe restriction of access on this facial record, and medical responsibility and informed deliberation reasonably support these requirements. The contrary applications of Akron are displaced to that extent. Accurate clinical and ancillary information receives its separately stated protection and limits. The Court does not retain a categorical prohibition of the concise accurate notices merely because they may favor childbirth. Its spousal-notice holding, however, forbids the demonstrated transfer of effective control over the protected decision and the dependent notice-compliance reports. Each reporting and parental component must be applied at its own stated scope.

No complete general formula has five joins. Stone proposes an independently impermissible substantial obstacle to protected, medically appropriate previability care and also a meaningful-burden inquiry requiring State-substantiated justification and fit. Stevens joins the substantive first five steps, not the generalized sixth-step facial rule; agreement there is not agreement with every proposed remedy. Blackmun would retain stricter received scrutiny. Souter does not adopt a comprehensive replacement, and O’Connor retains a severity-first inquiry with a possible compelling justification outside independently forbidden control. Kennedy adopts the specific constitutional boundaries and permissions without a universal scale. White, Scalia and Thomas would replace fundamental-right analysis with rational review, but three votes cannot make that the general rule either.

Marks v. United States permits identification of a controlling narrower ground in an appropriate fractured judgment. It does not permit a court to assemble a new formula from incompatible parts. Stone’s invalidating rule is not a logical subset of O’Connor’s: a meaningful lesser burden can fail his justification requirement while passing her reasonable-relation inquiry, and she may accept a compelling justification he would reject for a substantial obstacle to protected care. Comparing permissions might produce a narrower overlap, but his delivery and waiting votes dissent from the sustaining judgments and cannot supply their controlling rationale. Blackmun’s stricter scrutiny may invalidate regulations Stone would permit; those compatible invalidating writers still lack five votes for a complete rule. O’Connor’s general permission and the three rational-review writers likewise supply only four; Kennedy’s particular permissions cannot be expanded into the missing comprehensive fifth rationale. On the actual sustaining components, five Justices directly join the narrow permission, so no Marks construction is needed to supply their authority.

The general substantial-obstacle formulation is likewise not a Court-wide replacement. Descriptive agreement that a particular obstacle is forbidden does not establish that every regulation below that threshold is valid. No joint opinion supplies a five-Justice general substantial-obstacle replacement. Outside the precise changes announced here, surviving Roe, Doe, Danforth, Akron, Thornburgh and the applicable minors’ precedents retain their actual force. Courts must identify their relevant holding, the challenged obligation, its record and the scope of relief. This decision supplies no universal numerical facial threshold or new proof quantum. Where its competing general approaches do not establish a rule beyond those authorities and the direct holdings, that question remains unresolved; the judgment does not authorize invention of a unified test.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Roe v. Wade, 410 U.S. 113 — Core protection retained; the trimester timetable no longer determines when health and potential-life regulation may begin.
- Akron, 462 U.S. 416 — Its contrary physician-delivery and fixed-delay applications are displaced to the extent of the direct permissions here; other actual holdings remain.
- Thornburgh, 476 U.S. 747 — Categorical rejection of these concise accurate ancillary notices because they may favor childbirth is displaced; protection against coercion, distortion and unlawful medical interference remains.
- Doe — Its protection of individualized clinical judgment retains its actual force; limited reporting permissions do not create comprehensive immunity or a general replacement test.
- Danforth — Its informed-consent, anti-veto and reporting holdings remain at their actual scopes and supply the specifically identified principles here.
- Bellotti — Its independent maturity/informed-consent or best-interests authorization safeguards retain their force in the minors’ setting.
- Ashcroft — Its parental-consent and adequate alternative-authorization holding remains; this decision does not replace it with a general abortion formula.
- Hodgson — Its parental-involvement holdings and distinct opinion coalitions retain their actual force; no universal regulatory test is inferred.
- Ohio v. Akron Center — Its actual bypass safeguards and facial-review holding are not generally replaced; Stone’s proposed broader facial formulation is noncontrolling.
- Marks v. United States, 430 U.S. 188 — Applied to examine the actual rationales and participating votes; no logical subset supplies a complete majority replacement framework.

### Stare decisis and the constitutional foundation

**Holding and operative rule.** A renewed disagreement with Roe's constitutional conclusion, without a sufficient reason to depart from precedent, does not justify withdrawing its workable protected-choice, viability and life-and-health boundaries. The absence of doctrinal or factual displacement and reliance in reproductive planning support retaining that foundation, while a specific defect in a separable regulatory rule may justify changing that rule without overruling the foundation.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Stare decisis and the constitutional foundation,” joined at this scope by Stone, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter: 6 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Ordinary precedent grounds for retaining the foundation.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court retains Roe's constitutional foundation because the grounds offered for abandoning it do not justify withdrawing an established liberty. Stare decisis means that a decided legal question carries weight when the Court is asked to decide it again. It does not make every earlier sentence permanent. It requires the Court to identify what rule it would change, why that rule can no longer fairly govern, and what would be disturbed by the change. Mere renewed disagreement with the original decision does not perform that work. A change in the membership of the Court supplies no substitute for a legal reason.

The protected-choice boundary remains workable. Courts can distinguish a prohibition of previability abortion from regulation of the conditions under which lawful care is provided. They can also distinguish regulation from the delegation of practical control over a competent adult's decision. Hard applications do not establish that the boundary has ceased to function. The disagreements in this case concern particular disclosures, delivery arrangements, delays and reporting duties. Those disagreements call for careful identification of the applicable rule; they do not show that constitutional protection of the decision itself is incapable of judicial application.

No intervening controlling decision has removed the foundation on which Roe rests. Griswold, Eisenstadt and Carey continue to protect reproductive decisions and individual autonomy in their respective settings. Webster did not yield a majority overruling Roe's central holding. The parental-involvement decisions recognize distinct interests concerning minors and evaluate the adequacy of an independent alternative; they do not confer comparable authority on an adult spouse. Treating those decisions as a silent repudiation of the underlying liberty would confuse a permitted regulation with abandonment of the interest it regulates. The Court therefore rejects the argument that precedent has already accomplished the overruling now requested.

Nor do the medical facts offered here eliminate the significance of viability or the need to protect necessary care. Medical developments may matter to the justification for a particular clinical regulation and to the suitability of an earlier mechanical rule. They do not establish that the patient's protected decision has become a matter of unrestricted legislative choice. Roe's implementation and its constitutional foundation are separable questions. The Court's precise treatment of trimester machinery, required information, physician delivery and waiting appears in the governing-standard and provision holdings. The reasoning for retaining the foundation does not silently resolve the different coalitions on those questions.

Reliance also extends beyond transactions documented by a contract or a purchased asset. People organize relationships, education, work and family life against a settled ability to make reproductive decisions and obtain lawful care. The Court need not invent a numerical estimate of that reliance to recognize its legal importance. This is not a guarantee that every existing clinical or regulatory arrangement will remain unchanged. An alteration must receive its own analysis of the rule affected, its justification and the resulting remedy. The Court's actual decisions in Hilton and Quill illustrate that adherence and correction require attention to the particular proposition and setting; their statutory and commercial contexts do not dictate the abortion result.

These considerations are sufficient. The Court adopts no independent rule giving a decision extra authority merely because public disagreement is intense, and no rule insulating constitutional error to preserve institutional standing. The obligation is to give a principled account of continuity and change. Here, the arguments for abandoning the protected-choice foundation do not establish unworkability, doctrinal displacement or a factual transformation sufficient to overcome the reasons for retaining it. The specific regulatory holdings remain subject to their separately stated reasoning and limits.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Roe v. Wade, 410 U.S. 113 — Retained at the protected-choice, viability and necessary life-and-health boundaries; separable implementation receives separate treatment.
- Griswold — Their reproductive-liberty premises remain in force and support continuity rather than doctrinal displacement.
- Eisenstadt — Their reproductive-liberty premises remain in force and support continuity rather than doctrinal displacement.
- Carey — Their reproductive-liberty premises remain in force and support continuity rather than doctrinal displacement.
- Webster v. Reproductive Health Services, 492 U.S. 490 — Distinguished from an overruling of Roe's foundation; no majority did so.
- Hilton v. South Carolina Public Railways Commission, OT1991, December 16, 1991 — This Term’s decision illustrates proposition-specific statutory precedent analysis; it does not dictate constitutional adherence here.
- Quill Corp. v. North Dakota, OT1991, May 26, 1992 — This Term’s decision illustrates separable precedent treatment and setting-specific reliance; its commercial and congressional considerations are not transplanted as abortion rules.

### The trimester timetable and the timing of regulation

**Holding and operative rule.** Roe’s trimester timetable no longer determines when health and potential-life regulation may begin; a regulation is not invalid solely because it operates in the first trimester. The State may pursue those interests throughout pregnancy, subject to independently applicable constitutional limitations.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “The trimester timetable and the timing of regulation,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas: 6 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Operative timing change, severed from constitutional-foundation and complete-framework questions.

**Controlling explanation.** Roe supplied a timetable that restricted when maternal-health and potential-life interests could support regulation. The Court removes that timetable as an independent constitutional barrier. It does not hold that every regulation is valid, or that every interest automatically outweighs an independently protected liberty. Danforth's approval of voluntary and informed consent already showed that the first trimester did not exclude every lawful medical requirement. The present change is broader and express: timing alone no longer prevents pursuit of maternal health and potential life throughout pregnancy. The validity of a particular law must therefore be decided under the constitutional rules applicable to that law, without treating its first-trimester operation as sufficient to invalidate it. This proposition neither identifies a complete substitute test nor joins every rationale offered for retaining or rejecting Roe's constitutional foundation. It states the point of operative agreement and leaves other disagreements to their actual opinion and proposition coalitions.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Roe v. Wade, 410 U.S. 113 — Its timetable no longer determines when health and potential-life regulation may begin; retained constitutional boundaries have their separate coalition.
- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Its consent permission shows that first-trimester care was never immune from every lawful medical rule; the present timing change is broader and express.

### Medical emergency and timely clinical judgment

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3203 survives the present facial challenge under the protective construction that compliance with the Act may not pose a significant threat to the woman's life or health. The physician may act on timely, good-faith clinical judgment before the danger becomes catastrophic, including in the disputed preeclampsia, inevitable-abortion and premature-membrane-rupture circumstances.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Medical emergency and timely clinical judgment,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Prospective statutory construction only; no independent vagueness holding.

**Statutory terms and source.** Statutory text: §3203 covers a condition which, on the physician's good-faith clinical judgment, necessitates immediate abortion to avert death or for which delay creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Section 3205(b) requires the physician, if possible before the abortion, to inform the woman of the medical indications supporting the judgment that abortion is necessary to avert her death or to avert substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. The separate §3205(c) information-omission defense requires the physician to establish by a preponderance a reasonable belief that disclosure would severely adversely affect the patient's physical or mental health; it is not a general waiting exemption. Source: supplied statutory appendix and the lower courts' identification of §3205(c), correcting the appendix's OCR label without changing its text. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** The three conditions and their medical consequences come from district findings 152–174. Their inclusion through prospective assessment is the Third Circuit's construction, 947 F.2d 700–701, adopted here; it is not an earlier authoritative Pennsylvania state-court holding. No new patient diagnosis is found. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains the definition as construed, rather than treating the words as a command to wait for death or irreversible injury to become imminent. The district court identified conditions in which prompt care could be necessary before that point. The Third Circuit read serious risk prospectively and concluded that the legislature intended compliance to cause no significant threat to life or health. That reading answers the concrete medical objection presented here. It permits the physician to assess the developing condition through good-faith clinical judgment and encompasses the three disputed diagnoses without postponing treatment until shock, infection or permanent harm occurs.

Webster illustrates attention to the actual clinical duty when construing a medical requirement. Here, the accepted prospective construction supplies the answer; this permission adopts no comprehensive constitutional medical-usefulness test. The disclosure-specific defense does different work: it protects certain omissions of information, not delay of necessary care. The present judgment neither approves a contrary enforcement construction nor decides every possible degree of health risk. A standalone vagueness theory and its disputed preservation are not independently decided.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Webster v. Reproductive Health Services, 492 U.S. 490 — Applied for attention to the actual clinical obligation when construing a medical requirement; its disparate broader frameworks are not merged.
- Roe v. Wade, 410 U.S. 113 — Their retained necessary-life-and-health protection and clinical-judgment principles support the protected-choice coalition; a Justice who accepts only the statutory construction does not thereby join every constitutional premise.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — Their retained necessary-life-and-health protection and clinical-judgment principles support the protected-choice coalition; a Justice who accepts only the statutory construction does not thereby join every constitutional premise.

### Accurate material medical information, including gestational age

**Holding and operative rule.** The State may require accurate, nonmisleading information material to voluntary informed consent about the proposed procedure, relevant risks and alternatives, probable gestational age, and the medical risks of continuing pregnancy. Sustaining that content does not by itself sustain a particular speaker requirement or a fixed delay.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Accurate material medical information, including gestational age,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Accurate medical content, separated from its delivery and timing.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3205(a)(1)(i)–(iii) requires the performing or referring physician orally to provide procedure or treatment information, risks and alternatives a reasonable patient would consider material, probable gestational age when the abortion will occur, and childbirth medical risks, at least24 hours earlier. The medical emergency defined in§3203 excuses the ordinary process: good-faith clinical judgment finds immediate abortion necessary to avert death or delay creating serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Section 3205(c) separately protects omitted information on the physician's preponderance showing of reasonable belief in severely adverse physical or mental-health consequences. Source: supplied statute. Delivery and timing receive separate judgments. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** District findings 185–189 describe patient-specific informed consent and the inadequacy of printed or recorded information alone; findings 204–213 describe qualified counselors and existing physician contact. Those findings do not enact a universal physician-only rule. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court distinguishes the information a patient receives from the arrangements used to deliver it. A decision can be voluntary and informed while the State identifies facts that a reasonable patient would consider material. Danforth permits written informed consent; it does not make the physician's preferred description the exclusive permissible means of informing the patient. Procedure risks, reasonable alternatives, probable gestational age and childbirth risks can bear directly on the choice and on the care provided.

The district's findings support individualized discussion rather than the substitution of a pamphlet for clinical judgment. They do not establish that these medical subjects are irrelevant or that truthful information itself compels a different decision. The patient remains free to choose, and the clinician may explain qualifications relevant to her circumstances. The holding approves accurate material content, not falsehood, ideological assent or a guarantee that every form of communication is appropriate. The physician-delivery and waiting requirements are separately adjudicated, and the statute's emergency protection and information-omission defense retain their distinct functions.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Planned Parenthood of Central Missouri v. Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied for permissible informed-consent requirements while retaining the patient's decision.
- Akron v. Akron Center, 462 U.S. 416 — They do not compel invalidation of accurate material medical content merely because the State specifies it; delivery, timing and coercion questions remain separately controlled.
- Thornburgh v. American College, 476 U.S. 747 — They do not compel invalidation of accurate material medical content merely because the State specifies it; delivery, timing and coercion questions remain separately controlled.

### Notice that free state information is available

**Holding and operative rule.** The State may require a concise, accurate notice that it publishes information describing fetal development and agencies offering alternatives, that the patient has a right to review it, and that a copy is free if she chooses. The notice does not require endorsement of an agency, acceptance of a pamphlet, reading it or agreement with a governmental viewpoint.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Notice that free state information is available,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 8 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Concise factual availability notice.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3205(a)(2)(i) gives this notice duty to the performing or referring physician, or a qualified physician assistant, health-care practitioner, technician or social worker expressly delegated responsibility by either physician, at least24 hours earlier. The timing question is separately adjudicated. The ordinary process is excused by§3203 medical emergency: good-faith clinical judgment finds immediate abortion necessary to avert death or delay creating serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Under§3205(c), omitted information is protected on a physician's preponderance showing of reasonable belief disclosure would severely adversely affect physical or mental health. Source: supplied statute. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** District findings 181–184 identify omitted agencies and some listed agencies supplying misinformation or inflammatory material, while allowing clinicians to recommend avoiding a listed agency, recommend another and correct limitations. They do not find every state statement false. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains the notice of availability as a distinct obligation. Knowing that information and assistance can be obtained may help a patient evaluate the available choices. The notice communicates that the State publishes materials and that the patient may obtain them without charge; it does not require the clinician to endorse every agency or the patient to accept the materials. Danforth permits informed-consent administration, and does not transfer the patient's decision.

The directory findings require care in describing what has been approved. Some agencies were omitted and some listed organizations supplied misinformation. Those facts do not make the fact of availability false, nor do they establish that every printed statement is inaccurate. Clinicians may explain defects and recommend other sources. Akron and Thornburgh do not require categorical invalidation of a truthful availability notice merely because the information may favor childbirth. This holding does not adjudicate every particular pamphlet as accurate, resolve a distinct compulsory-distribution challenge by label, or merge notice content with the separately decided timing requirement.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to factual informed-consent administration without transferring the decision.
- Akron, 462 U.S. 416 — Qualified against categorical invalidation of truthful availability notices merely because the information may favor childbirth; protection against actual coercion or falsehood remains.
- Thornburgh, 476 U.S. 747 — Qualified against categorical invalidation of truthful availability notices merely because the information may favor childbirth; protection against actual coercion or falsehood remains.

### Notice of potentially available medical assistance

**Holding and operative rule.** The State may require accurate notice that medical assistance benefits may be available for prenatal care, childbirth and neonatal care, with more detailed information available in the state materials. The notice must not represent conditional eligibility as a guarantee that benefits will be paid.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Notice of potentially available medical assistance,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 8 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Conditional assistance information.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3205(a)(2)(ii) permits the performing/referring physician or a qualified physician assistant, health-care practitioner, technician or social worker delegated by either physician to give the notice; its24-hour timing is separately adjudicated. Section 3203 excuses the ordinary process for good-faith clinical judgment requiring immediate abortion to avert death or where delay creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Section 3205(c) independently protects omitted information on a physician's preponderance showing of reasonable belief in severely adverse physical or mental-health effects. Source: supplied statute. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** The conditional word 'may' is statutory text. Neither the statute nor this record supplies a finding that every patient qualifies for benefits or that those benefits will be sufficient for her circumstances. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains the assistance notice because it conveys a limited factual proposition relevant to the choices before the patient. The statute says benefits may be available. It does not promise eligibility or payment, require the patient to apply, or make her choice depend on an official's assessment of her financial reasons. The clinician may explain actual eligibility limits and other relevant circumstances.

The principal objection treats the notice as invalid because it may encourage childbirth. That is not enough to invalidate accurate information about an available option. The State's legitimate interest in potential life permits lawful information while leaving the patient free to decide. The holding does not depend on treating every professional communication as commercial speech or on finding all state materials accurate. It sustains the conditional notice itself, with the medical-emergency protection and separate information-omission defense intact. Delivery timing, chosen receipt of materials and any proved compulsory misinformation receive their own analysis rather than being resolved by the notice's subject matter.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied for lawful informed-consent administration.
- Webster, 492 U.S. 490 — Its recognition of lawful governmental childbirth preference supports truthful information, without importing an affirmative funding entitlement or a comprehensive abortion standard.
- Akron, 462 U.S. 416 — Qualified to the extent a categorical anti-persuasion rule would invalidate this accurate conditional notice alone.
- Thornburgh, 476 U.S. 747 — Qualified to the extent a categorical anti-persuasion rule would invalidate this accurate conditional notice alone.

### Paternal-support information and the rape omission permission

**Holding and operative rule.** The State may require an accurate statement that the father is legally liable to assist in supporting the child even if he offered to pay for an abortion, without representing that liability as guaranteed payment. The statute permits the information to be omitted in a rape case and imposes no law-enforcement-reporting condition on that permission.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Paternal-support information and the rape omission permission,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 8 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Accurate support liability and the rape omission permission.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3205(a)(2)(iii) states: 'In the case of rape, this information may be omitted.' The performing/referring physician or a qualified physician assistant, health-care practitioner, technician or social worker delegated by either physician may provide the notice. Its24-hour timing is separately adjudicated. Section 3203 excuses the ordinary process where good-faith clinical judgment requires immediate abortion to avert death or delay creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Section 3205(c) separately protects omission on a physician's preponderance showing of reasonable belief disclosure would severely adversely affect physical or mental health. Source: supplied statute. None of these information protections is the§3209 reported-spousal-assault route. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** Legal liability and permission to omit are statutory commands/qualifications. Ability to collect actual support is a different practical matter, not guaranteed by the statutory notice or newly found by the Court. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains the notice as a statement of legal responsibility, not as a promise that the patient will receive money. A father's willingness to finance an abortion does not remove the legal support obligation described by the statute. That information can bear on an informed decision, while the clinician remains free to explain practical uncertainty or inability to collect.

The rape sentence must be applied according to its terms. The information may be omitted; the statute does not require omission. It also does not require a police report before the patient can receive that protection. The distinct reported-spousal-assault condition belongs to the notice provision and cannot be imported here. The separate severe-health-effect information defense remains available on its own statutory showing. Truthful legal information does not become unconstitutional solely because it could influence a patient toward childbirth. This holding does not require ideological assent, guarantee the truth of every pamphlet, or settle the independently contested delivery and waiting mechanisms.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to information relevant to voluntary informed consent.
- Akron, 462 U.S. 416 — Qualified against categorical invalidation of this accurate legal notice solely because it may favor childbirth; coercion and misinformation remain distinct.
- Thornburgh, 476 U.S. 747 — Qualified against categorical invalidation of this accurate legal notice solely because it may favor childbirth; coercion and misinformation remain distinct.

### Lawful publication and the patient’s choice to receive materials

**Holding and operative rule.** The State may publish lawful, accurate information concerning pregnancy, childbirth, assistance and alternatives, and a patient may choose to request and receive it. The provisions before the Court do not compel her to accept, read or endorse the materials; this permission does not decide the separate validity of every required notice or distribution duty.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Lawful publication and the patient’s choice to receive materials,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Lawful publication and genuinely elective receipt.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3205(a)(2)(i) describes the woman's right to review the State's printed materials and obtain a free copy if she chooses;§3205(a)(3) supplies a copy if she chooses to view it. Section 3208 requires accessible publications, annual updating, objective/nonjudgmental scientific information, realistic development illustrations appropriate to pregnancy stage and free distribution on request. Source: supplied statutory text. The permission to publish and elect receipt is separate from the adjudication of compulsory clinician duties, delivery timing and particular challenged content. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** The optional character of acceptance and reading comes from the statutory text. Findings181–184 about directory omissions and some agencies' misconduct are record evidence, not a finding every pamphlet is false or a judicial certification that every publication is accurate. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court distinguishes the State's ability to publish lawful information and the patient's choice to obtain it from the separate question whether a clinician may be compelled to communicate a particular message. Publication alone does not transfer the treatment decision, and a patient may consider information about pregnancy, assistance or alternatives without surrendering her authority to accept or reject it. The statute makes the provision of a copy depend on her choice. It does not require acceptance, reading or agreement.

This limited permission neither certifies the accuracy of every existing document nor immunizes a required false or ideological communication. The statutory accuracy standards and the trial's specific directory findings retain their separate roles. Disagreement over a mandatory notice or delivery requirement does not make lawful publication itself forbidden. Conversely, recognition that the State may publish does not resolve those compulsory obligations. The Court therefore states the elective-publication proposition separately and leaves each challenged duty and requested focused remedy to its own holding.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Webster v. Reproductive Health Services, 492 U.S. 490 — Its recognition of lawful governmental preference is consistent with publishing lawful information; compulsory private communication remains a separate question.
- Wooley v. Maynard, 430 U.S. 705 — Protection against compelled ideological expression is not displaced by permission for lawful government publication and elective receipt.

### The requested focused proceedings concerning particular materials
