**Holding and operative rule.** The particular-materials objection does not justify the additional focused vacatur, remand or interim distribution order requested here. This disposition does not certify every pamphlet as accurate or itself resolve distinct objections to compulsory notices and their delivery; those components have their own judgments.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “The requested focused proceedings concerning particular materials,” joined at this scope by White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 8 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Present record does not warrant focused vacatur or interim suspension; no blanket accuracy finding.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3205(a)(3) supplies a copy only if the woman chooses to view the materials. Section 3208 requires accessible publications, annual updates, objective/nonjudgmental scientific information, realistic development illustrations appropriate to pregnancy stage and free distribution on request. Section 3203 excuses the ordinary process for good-faith clinical judgment requiring immediate abortion to avert death or delay creating serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. Section 3205(c) separately protects omitted information upon a physician's preponderance showing of reasonable belief in severely adverse physical or mental-health effects. Source: supplied statute. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** District findings 181–184 concern directory omissions, some listed agencies' misinformation/inflammatory materials, clinician advice against an agency and potential supplementation. The statute's accuracy requirement is not itself proof of universal actual compliance. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court declines the requested additional focused proceedings on the particular-materials objection. The actual findings identify omissions in an agency directory and misinformation or inflammatory literature supplied by some listed organizations. They also establish that clinicians may explain defects, recommend avoiding an agency and suggest other assistance. Those findings must be respected, but a listed organization's conduct is not automatically a false statement in every state publication.

The statute's demand for objective and accurate materials is likewise not proof that each publication complies. The Court gives no categorical factual approval. The present record nevertheless does not justify the proposed focused vacatur or interim distribution order on this distinct objection. This conclusion does not convert elective receipt into mandatory reading or endorsement, and it does not decide another component's compulsory-notice claim by implication. A concrete unlawful communication or application remains subject to its proper constitutional analysis and remedy. The holding concerns the additional proceedings requested on this record, not immunity for future misinformation.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied for reasonable informed-consent administration that preserves the patient's decision.
- Thornburgh, 476 U.S. 747 — Its protection against compulsory misleading or coercive communication is not treated as categorical invalidity of chosen factual materials.
- Wooley v. Maynard, 430 U.S. 705 — The distinct protection against compelled ideological expression is preserved; no general professional-speech immunity is adopted.

### Physician delivery of the specified medical information

**Holding and operative rule.** Pennsylvania may require the performing or referring physician to deliver orally the accurate medical information specified in section 3205(a)(1): the procedure and its risks and alternatives material to a reasonable patient, probable gestational age, and the medical risks of carrying the pregnancy to term. This permission concerns the designated speaker and the present facial record; the fixed timing receives a separate holding.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “Physician delivery of the specified medical information,” joined at this scope by White, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas: 5 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Sufficient permission for this physician responsibility on this facial record.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3203 means a condition which, on the physician's good-faith clinical judgment, necessitates immediate abortion to avert death or for which delay creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. The prospective construction covers preeclampsia, inevitable abortion and premature membrane rupture at onset without waiting for irreversible injury. Under section 3205(c), no physician is guilty for failing to furnish subsection (a) information if the physician demonstrates by a preponderance of the evidence a reasonable belief that furnishing it would have resulted in a severely adverse effect on the patient's physical or mental health. This is a disclosure defense, not a general exemption from waiting. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** Statutory text: supplied statutory-only appendix, section 3205(a)(1), section 3203 and section 3205(c), the latter label verified by the full lower opinions. Record findings: district findings 185–189 and 204–213 support patient-specific dialogue, counselor competence, ordinary physician contact and additional physician-delivery costs. They do not establish an express parent-specific delivery command under section 3206. The severe-restriction conclusion is limited to this facial record; it does not mean absolute impossibility is the only burden that can matter or that no individual ever faces prevention. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the designated physician may be made responsible for these medical disclosures. Danforth recognizes the legitimacy of voluntary and informed consent; accurate information about the procedure, material risks and alternatives, gestational age and childbirth risks serves that purpose. The record establishes that qualified counselors can communicate effectively and that requiring earlier physician contact adds expense and scheduling difficulties. The Court accepts those findings. They do not establish severe restriction of access on this facial record, and the State may reasonably connect personal physician delivery to medical responsibility. This conclusion does not depend on finding counselors incompetent or the physician requirement medically indispensable. Akron's categorical rejection of physician-only delivery does not govern this requirement after today's change. The permission also does not classify all compensated medical speech as commercial: Riley's protection of expression remains, and these specific disclosures are tied to an informed medical decision. The Court decides this speaker requirement on these grounds, leaving other professional-speech restrictions and any comprehensive test for abortion regulation outside this holding.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — applies its recognition that voluntary, informed consent is a legitimate requirement.
- Akron — displaces its categorical bar to physician-only delivery at this specified medical-information scope.
- Riley — preserves independent speech protection; compensation alone does not make professional expression commercial.

### The fixed twenty-four-hour interval

**Holding and operative rule.** Pennsylvania may require the section 3205(a)(1) and (a)(2) information to be supplied at least twenty-four hours before the abortion on this facial record. This is a permission for the actual interval, not a new parent-specific waiting period under section 3206 or a rule governing every delay.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “The fixed twenty-four-hour interval,” joined at this scope by White, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas: 5 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Sufficient permission for this deliberation interval on this facial record.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3203 means a condition which, on the physician's good-faith clinical judgment, necessitates immediate abortion to avert death or for which delay creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. The prospective construction covers preeclampsia, inevitable abortion and premature membrane rupture at onset without waiting for irreversible injury. Section 3205(b) requires the physician, if possible before the abortion, to inform the woman of the medical indications supporting the judgment that abortion is necessary to avert her death or to avert substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. Under section 3205(c), no physician is guilty for failing to furnish subsection (a) information if the physician demonstrates by a preponderance of the evidence a reasonable belief that furnishing it would have resulted in a severely adverse effect on the patient's physical or mental health. This is a disclosure defense, not a general exemption from waiting. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** Statutory text: supplied appendix, section 3205(a)(1)–(2), section 3203, section 3205(b) and verified section 3205(c). Record findings: district findings 190–203 supply actual repeated travel, scheduling delays often longer than twenty-four hours, expense, exposure and later-procedure risk. Their absence of medical necessity does not negate the separately assessed deliberation purpose. Parental-content treatment is a lower-court construction; the statute does not expressly incorporate every section 3205 timing mechanism as a parent-specific requirement. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that this interval may provide time for informed deliberation. Danforth recognizes that a woman's consent should reflect knowledge of the decision's nature and consequences. That legitimate purpose is distinct from a claim that waiting itself improves medical treatment. The District Court found no medical need for a fixed delay and documented additional trips, travel and lodging costs, lost wages, child care, scheduling delays and later-procedure risks, falling especially heavily on disadvantaged patients. Those findings are accepted. On this facial record, however, they do not establish severe restriction of access, and the interval reasonably relates to reflection after the required information is supplied. Akron's categorical rejection of this waiting requirement therefore no longer controls. The statutory prospective emergency rule preserves immediate treatment at its stated clinical threshold; the separate disclosure-health defense does not itself excuse waiting. The Court does not declare all delays reasonable, require women to prove absolute impossibility, or adopt a comprehensive threshold for other abortion regulations. It sustains this interval on the stated record and grounds.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth — applies the legitimate interest in voluntary and informed consent, without attributing approval of every fixed delay to that decision.
- Akron — displaces its categorical rejection of this twenty-four-hour requirement at the present scope; no general approval of all waiting periods follows.

### Written certification of lawful receipt

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3205(a)(4) may require written certification, before the abortion, that the information lawfully required under paragraphs (1), (2) and (3) has been supplied. Certification records receipt, not agreement with a viewpoint or receipt of materials the patient did not choose; its timing and delivery links have only the authority of the separate judgments sustaining those requirements.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Written certification of lawful receipt,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Lawful receipt documentation only; ancillary and timing/delivery links carry their own coalitions. Ancillary content links have eight direct supporters (all except Blackmun); actual delivery/timing links have White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas only. The common receipt rule does not combine those counts.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3205(a)(4) refers to information required under paragraphs (1), (2) and (3), the last requiring a copy only if the woman chooses to view it. Section 3205(c) makes violations unprofessional conduct subject to suspension or revocation through applicable procedures; performance without the certificate, or with knowledge or reason to know that informed consent was not obtained, is a summary offense initially and a third-degree misdemeanor subsequently. The information-omission defense requires a physician’s preponderance proof of reasonable belief that furnishing information would severely adversely affect physical or mental health. Section 3203’s separate good-faith emergency requires immediate abortion to avert death or involves delay creating serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. Sources: supplied statute and lower-court identification of the penalty paragraph as (c), despite the supplied OCR label. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court permits a written record that the patient received the information the law validly requires. Danforth upheld written informed consent because recording a voluntary decision serves a legitimate medical function. A receipt certificate likewise documents communication; it does not require agreement with the State’s preference or a statement that the patient read a publication she declined. The reference to paragraph (3) follows that paragraph’s condition of elective receipt. The certificate cannot expand it into compulsory reading.

The linked requirements must still be counted separately. All nine accept ordinary lawful receipt documentation; eight sustain the ancillary content links, and five sustain the challenged delivery and waiting links. The certificate supplies no additional vote for any of those requirements. Nor may dependent sanctions enforce a condition that this judgment invalidates. The statutory emergency protection, information-omission defense and actual culpability requirements remain operative in their own terms. The Court decides the present consent scheme and directs conformity to the surviving obligations, without granting a general authority to compel ideological certification.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to lawful written informed consent and its documentation; no ideological-assent requirement follows.
- The information, delivery and waiting holdings in this decision — Control the exact certificate links; documentation does not independently validate a disputed underlying obligation.

### The independent speech objection to medical-consent information

**Holding and operative rule.** The First Amendment does not invalidate these accurate, nonmisleading disclosures about material procedure risks and alternatives, probable gestational age and childbirth risks in the patient’s actual consent process. This permission depends on their factual, consent-related character and does not classify all professional speech as commercial or authorize ideological affirmation.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “The independent speech objection to medical-consent information,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Independent, narrow factual medical-consent First Amendment ground.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court considers the providers’ speech claim independently of the patient’s abortion-liberty claim. The information at issue describes the treatment, material alternatives and risks, the pregnancy’s probable stage and the risks of continuing it. Those subjects bear directly on understanding the proposed medical service. Danforth recognizes lawful informed-consent administration; that setting supports these specific disclosures without creating an exception to the First Amendment for everything said by a clinician.

Riley rejects the proposition that compensation alone reduces protected expression to commercial speech. Wooley forbids compelled ideological affirmation. Neither proposition is abandoned here. The required medical facts do not demand a profession of belief that abortion is wrong, agreement with a government viewpoint, or suppression of clinical qualifications. The contrary argument would make these ordinary factual consent disclosures impermissible solely because the State requires them. Their actual consent connection defeats that facial objection. The holding leaves different ideological or misleading commands subject to their own review and does not decide the separate delivery and waiting burdens through a speech label.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Supplies the legitimate consent setting, not a universal speech exemption.
- Riley v. National Federation of the Blind, 487 U.S. 781 — Compensation alone does not classify all clinical speech as commercial; that limitation remains.
- Wooley v. Maynard, 430 U.S. 705 — Protection against ideological affirmation retained; these factual consent disclosures do not require it.

### The independent speech objection to concise ancillary notices

**Holding and operative rule.** The First Amendment does not facially invalidate the concise, accurate notices of available materials, potentially available assistance and paternal legal responsibility as limited information about the patient’s options. Their validity does not authorize compelled endorsement, misleading statements, compulsory reading or a categorical reduction of professional speech protection.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “The independent speech objection to concise ancillary notices,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 8 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Independent, narrow factual ancillary-notice First Amendment ground; Blackmun does not reach this additional ground.

**Statutory terms and source.** Under §3205(a)(2), the performing or referring physician or a qualified physician assistant, health-care practitioner, technician or social worker delegated by either physician may convey the notices. Materials are free and supplied if the patient chooses; assistance may be available, and paternal liability is not guaranteed collection. In rape, paternal-support information may be omitted without police reporting (§3205(a)(2)(iii)). The §3205(c) omission defense requires the physician’s preponderance showing of reasonable belief in severely adverse physical or mental-health effects of furnishing information; it does not waive waiting. Section 3203 separately excuses ordinary process on good-faith clinical judgment requiring immediate abortion to avert death or involving delay that creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. Source: supplied statute. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains these limited notices against the independent facial speech objection. They convey the availability of an informational option, the possibility of assistance and a legal support responsibility. They need not represent an agency as reliable, benefits as guaranteed or legal liability as certain payment. The patient may decline the printed materials, and clinicians remain free to explain qualifications and correct misinformation. Those features connect the brief factual notices to an informed choice without demanding personal endorsement of the State’s preference.

Riley requires attention to the actual communication rather than a shortcut based on compensation. Wooley preserves the distinction between supplying facts and affirming an ideology. The notices sustained here fall on the factual side of that distinction for the limited reasons stated; truth alone is not a universal defense to compelled speech. The Court does not approve every publication or dispose of a materially different communication requirement by describing it as professional regulation. Blackmun does not reach this additional speech ground against ancillary duties he would invalidate under abortion liberty.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Riley, 487 U.S. 781 — Applied to reject a compensation-based shortcut while assessing the actual required communication.
- Wooley, 430 U.S. 705 — Ideological-affirmation protection retained.
- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Provides the informed-choice setting; no categorical professional-speech immunity follows.

### One-parent informed consent and independent judicial authorization

**Holding and operative rule.** A State may provide for one-parent informed consent for an unemancipated minor's abortion when an effective confidential and expeditious judicial alternative independently authorizes care upon maturity, capacity and actual informed consent, or upon best interests. The minor need not first ask a parent, and necessary emergency care cannot await either route.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “One-parent informed consent and independent judicial authorization,” joined at this scope by Stone, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter: 6 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Constitutionally necessary independent authorization safeguards; three additional judgment votes do not join necessity. White, Scalia and Thomas sustain the architecture on their separately stated regulatory grounds; the basic judgment is unanimous, but their votes do not adopt the constitutional necessity of the safeguards.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3206 applies to unemancipated minors under 18; its separate adjudged-incompetent guardian rule is not independently adjudicated. In the minor-consent route the physician first obtains informed consent from both the pregnant minor and one parent. A parent or guardian considers only the child's or ward's best interests. When pregnancy results from incest and the father is a party to the incestuous act, only the mother's parental consent is needed; the minor's own informed consent remains required. If both parents have died or are otherwise unavailable to the physician within a reasonable time and in a reasonable manner, guardian consent suffices; if parents are divorced, custodial-parent consent suffices; if neither parent nor legal guardian is available to the physician within a reasonable time and in a reasonable manner, an adult standing in place of a parent may consent. The minor may elect judicial authorization without first seeking parental consent, in the court of common pleas of the judicial district where she resides or where abortion is sought. Maturity/capacity and actual informed consent, or alternatively best interests, authorize care. Counsel is supplied unless she chooses private counsel or knowingly/intelligently waives representation; proceedings and records are confidential, her name is excluded from a public docket, the trial court must rule within3 business days of application and the appellate court within5 business days after filing of the appeal. Section 3203 medical emergency excuses: good-faith clinical judgment finds immediate abortion necessary to avert death or delay creating serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Sources: supplied§§3203,3206 and Third Circuit statutory quotations of(f),(h). See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** The statutory alternatives are legal commands. The record supplies no current failure of bypass courts to function, district finding 266. Additional parental attendance is a distinct lower-court operation/construction addressed separately. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court preserves the permissible parental structure and the independent route to judicial authorization. Bellotti requires an alternative that respects a minor's maturity and best interests rather than giving a parent an absolute veto. Hodgson and Ohio v. Akron Center confirm the significance of adequate safeguards when the State seeks parental participation. A parent's responsibility for a minor supplies an interest different from a spouse's relationship to a competent adult.

The alternatives here are genuine legal routes, not cumulative obstacles. A mature applicant who has actually consented need not additionally prove that abortion serves her best interests, and an applicant may proceed through best interests without establishing maturity. The statute permits direct resort to court and supplies counsel, confidentiality and expedition. The current trial record does not establish that the courts are unprepared to operate that process. This holding does not promise that future maladministration is immune from review, require a prior request to a parent, or decide an unpresented adult-incapacity claim. The separate attendance objection receives its own disposition.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Bellotti v. Baird, 443 U.S. 622 — Applied for independent maturity/informed-consent or best-interests authorization and effective procedural safeguards.
- Hodgson v. Minnesota, 497 U.S. 417 — Applied for the validity of parental involvement coupled with an adequate bypass, without attributing all separate rationales to the same coalition.
- Ohio v. Akron Center, 497 U.S. 502 — Applied to confidentiality, expedition and actual bypass operation; hypothetical administrative failure alone is insufficient.
- Planned Parenthood Association v. Ashcroft, 462 U.S. 476 — Applied for a parental-consent system with constitutionally sufficient alternative authorization.

### Accurate information supporting informed parental consent

**Holding and operative rule.** Permissible parental involvement may be informed by accurate medical information about the procedure, material risks and alternatives, probable gestational age and continuing pregnancy, together with accurate factual information about assistance, support and available materials. This does not incorporate every§3205 timing or speaker mechanism into§3206 or preserve an otherwise invalid obligation merely because it is used in parental counseling.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Accurate information supporting informed parental consent,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 8 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Lawful informed-parent content at the actual lower-court construction’s scope.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3206 applies to unemancipated minors under 18; its separate adjudged-incompetent guardian rule is not independently adjudicated. In the minor-consent route the physician first obtains informed consent from both the pregnant minor and one parent. A parent or guardian considers only the child's or ward's best interests. When pregnancy results from incest and the father is a party to the incestuous act, only the mother's parental consent is needed; the minor's own informed consent remains required. If both parents have died or are otherwise unavailable to the physician within a reasonable time and in a reasonable manner, guardian consent suffices; if parents are divorced, custodial-parent consent suffices; if neither parent nor legal guardian is available to the physician within a reasonable time and in a reasonable manner, an adult standing in place of a parent may consent. The minor may elect judicial authorization without first seeking parental consent, in the court of common pleas of the judicial district where she resides or where abortion is sought. Maturity/capacity and actual informed consent, or alternatively best interests, authorize care. Counsel is supplied unless she chooses private counsel or knowingly/intelligently waives representation; proceedings and records are confidential, her name is excluded from a public docket, the trial court must rule within3 business days of application and the appellate court within5 business days after filing of the appeal. Section 3203 medical emergency excuses: good-faith clinical judgment finds immediate abortion necessary to avert death or delay creating serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Sources: supplied§§3203,3206 and Third Circuit statutory quotations of(f),(h). See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** Section 3206 requires informed consent but contains no express wholesale incorporation of every§3205 mechanism. The district court at744 F.Supp.1382–1384 uses§3205 information to explain informed-parent content. Additional attendance is its distinct construction/operational finding, reserved by Third Circuit footnote 23. For paternal information§3205(a)(2)(iii) says,'In the case of rape, this information may be omitted,' without a reporting condition; legal liability is not guaranteed collection. This identifies the underlying patient-information provision's terms; it does not invent an express wholesale incorporation of its mechanisms into the parental statute. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains the ordinary informational content of permissible parental participation. A parent asked to consider only the child's best interests may reasonably receive accurate facts about the proposed procedure, relevant risks, alternatives, gestational age and continuing pregnancy. Accurate information about available assistance, legal support responsibilities and materials can also inform that consideration without requiring adoption of a moral viewpoint.

Bellotti and Hodgson recognize a legitimate role for informed adult assistance. This statutory scheme also supplies an independent authorization route; its constitutional necessity receives a separate holding. Information does not become unlawful solely because it could support a decision to continue pregnancy. The Court nevertheless separates content from the mechanisms used to convey it. The district's use of§3205 to explain informed consent does not insert a parent-specific waiting clock, a nondelegable physician-only task or an express clinic-visit sentence into§3206. Nor does preserving informed parental involvement revive a delivery, timing or content duty independently held invalid. The parental structure, its accurate information, its independent safeguards and the distinct attendance objection must each retain their own analysis.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Bellotti v. Baird, 443 U.S. 622 — Applied for permissible adult guidance with independent authorization safeguards.
- Hodgson v. Minnesota, 497 U.S. 417 — Applied to the legitimate minor-parent informational relationship without treating its separate opinions as one universal regulatory standard.
- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Informed-consent principles support accurate information while the particular duties and veto limits remain separate.

### The separate parental-attendance objection

**Holding and operative rule.** The actual facial record does not warrant an additional focused vacatur, remand or interim order on the parental-attendance objection to the permissible informed-parent and independent-authorization structure. The Court accepts the positive findings about intended attendance and resulting burdens, leaves the reserved Pennsylvania-law interpretation open, and does not hold that every unnecessary visit for an already-consenting parent is cured by offering bypass.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “The separate parental-attendance objection,” joined at this scope by White, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 8 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Limited facial-record and construction ground; no universal curative-bypass rule.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3206 applies to unemancipated minors under 18; its separate adjudged-incompetent guardian rule is not independently adjudicated. In the minor-consent route the physician first obtains informed consent from both the pregnant minor and one parent. A parent or guardian considers only the child's or ward's best interests. When pregnancy results from incest and the father is a party to the incestuous act, only the mother's parental consent is needed; the minor's own informed consent remains required. If both parents have died or are otherwise unavailable to the physician within a reasonable time and in a reasonable manner, guardian consent suffices; if parents are divorced, custodial-parent consent suffices; if neither parent nor legal guardian is available to the physician within a reasonable time and in a reasonable manner, an adult standing in place of a parent may consent. The minor may elect judicial authorization without first seeking parental consent, in the court of common pleas of the judicial district where she resides or where abortion is sought. Maturity/capacity and actual informed consent, or alternatively best interests, authorize care. Counsel is supplied unless she chooses private counsel or knowingly/intelligently waives representation; proceedings and records are confidential, her name is excluded from a public docket, the trial court must rule within3 business days of application and the appellate court within5 business days after filing of the appeal. Section 3203 medical emergency excuses: good-faith clinical judgment finds immediate abortion necessary to avert death or delay creating serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. Sources: supplied§§3203,3206 and Third Circuit statutory quotations of(f),(h). See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** District findings 232,238–259 describe intended face-to-face practice and the burden when a willing parent cannot or will not attend; district 1382–1384 uses§3205 content to explain informed parental consent. It does not establish express incorporation of every§3205 timing/delivery mechanism. Third Circuit footnote 23 leaves the attendance interpretation undecided. These are findings/constructions, not new statutory words. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court accepts the district's findings that a parent may agree yet be unable or unwilling to attend, and that an additional encounter can cause expense and delay. Those findings are not an express statutory visit sentence. The Third Circuit left the state-law interpretation open, and this decision does not turn that reservation into a finding that attendance can never be required.

The requested additional focused vacatur and interim order are not justified on this facial record. The permissible informed-parent structure remains accompanied by a genuine independent route to authorization, with the actual statutory safeguards assessed in light of Bellotti, Hodgson and Ohio v. Akron Center. The record does not establish that this route is unavailable or presently malfunctioning. That conclusion is confined to the request and evidence here. It does not announce that an adequate bypass cures every unjustified demand upon an already-consenting family. An actual application or authoritative construction imposing an unlawful additional condition remains challengeable. Nor does the Court create a parent-specific waiting period or nondelegable physician-delivery command.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Hodgson, 497 U.S. 417 — Applied for adequate independent authorization as a safeguard in the distinct parental-involvement setting; not a universal cure for needless attendance.
- Ohio v. Akron Center, 497 U.S. 502 — Applied to actual procedural safeguards and the insufficiency of hypothetical maladministration.
- Bellotti, 443 U.S. 622 — Its independent authorization safeguards remain; no prior parental-request requirement is added.

### Spousal notice and State-enabled control of protected choice

**Holding and operative rule.** The State may encourage voluntary marital consultation, but it may not condition protected abortion care on notice that gives a spouse practical control of the patient's decision in the relationships established by this record. Section 3209 and its dependent enforcement, together with§3214(a)(12)'s notice and nonnotice-reason requirements, are invalid; the holding does not announce a general numerical facial-challenge test or an independent equality or informational-privacy rule.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Spousal notice and State-enabled control of protected choice,” joined at this scope by Stone, Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter: 6 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Protected-choice prohibition of demonstrated practical spousal control.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3209 requires a signed statement, not notarization, that the married patient notified her spouse; it must warn that a false statement is punishable. A signed, nonnotarized substitute carrying the warning suffices if at least one condition is certified: spouse is not father; diligent effort failed to locate him; pregnancy resulted from§3128 spousal sexual assault reported to a law-enforcement agency with jurisdiction; or the woman has reason to believe notice likely will result in bodily injury to her by the spouse or another person. Section 3128(a)–(b), quoted in district 268, covers sexual/deviate intercourse through forcible compulsion, threat of forcible compulsion that would prevent resistance by a person of reasonable resolution or with an unconscious spouse; district 297 identifies the incorporated90-day reporting restriction. Medical emergency independently excuses:§3203 good-faith clinical judgment requires immediate abortion to avert death or delay creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function. The statute does not generally excuse psychological/economic harm, injury to children/others, publicity or divorce/custody retaliation. Violations constitute unprofessional conduct subject to license suspension/revocation through applicable procedures; Section 3209(e) also imposes civil liability to the spouse who is the father of the aborted child for any damages caused by the knowing violation and punitive damages of $5,000; the court must award a prevailing plaintiff a reasonable attorney fee as part of costs. Sources: supplied§§3203,3209 and district 268,297. The rape-information permission in§3205(a)(2)(iii) has no reporting condition and is a different provision. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** District findings 273–308 establish actual family dynamics, coercion and limitations of the substitute statements. The identification of the affected population and the conclusion that State-required disclosure enables practical control are judicial applications to those findings. No finding that all husbands are abusive, that every married patient is prevented from obtaining care, or that patient names become public is made. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that marriage does not give the State authority to place a competent adult's protected abortion decision under another person's practical control. The Commonwealth may encourage spouses to discuss pregnancy and may respect a husband's serious interest in potential parenthood. Those interests do not authorize a condition that, on this record, enables him to prevent his wife's exercise of the protected choice. The holding concerns the operation of this compulsory condition, not the value of voluntary consultation or the ordinary responsibilities of family life.

The inquiry begins with the people whose conduct the statute changes. Many married patients already consult their spouses. Their voluntary decisions do not answer the constitutional objection of patients whom the law compels to disclose. Nor may the relevant group be defined simply as everyone who ultimately proves a constitutional injury. The statutory condition and the record identify married patients who would withhold notice and whose access is made dependent on disclosure or an available substitute statement. The evidence about that operation supplies the basis for relief, without inventing a numerical cutoff for all facial challenges.

The trial findings establish more than inconvenience or disagreement. Some husbands use physical confinement, threats, control of money, psychological intimidation or threats concerning children to control a woman's actions. Compelled notice can expose the intended abortion and give that control immediate practical force. The fact that a private person supplies the threat does not remove the State's role. The law makes disclosure a prerequisite to treatment and backs it with consequences for the physician. Without the compulsory condition, the patient could seek care without supplying the information that enables the interference.

The statutory substitutes do not adequately remove this operation. Nonpaternity and inability to locate a spouse after diligent effort address different circumstances. The bodily-injury route concerns likely injury to the woman by the spouse or another person; it does not generally cover financial deprivation, psychological harm or injury threatened to children. The spousal-assault route requires a report to law enforcement and incorporates the90-day restriction identified in the district findings. Reporting itself may expose the woman to the danger she seeks to avoid. The record also explains why some abused women cannot realistically use a danger certification despite the threats they face. Medical emergency addresses necessary care under a separate clinical standard, not every form of coercive marital control.

Danforth supplies the governing distinction. The State cannot give a spouse an absolute veto over the patient's protected decision. A formal right to sign a refusal is not the only way to create that control. A legally compelled disclosure that predictably enables prevention of care can produce the same prohibited result. The State's strongest response—that notice differs from consent—therefore fails on the actual operation established here. Preserving the woman's decision does not deny a husband's concern; it denies the State power to enforce that concern by transferring control over her body and treatment.

Bellotti, Hodgson and Ohio v. Akron Center concern minors and the distinct responsibilities of parents, with independent authorization safeguards. They do not make an adult spouse a guardian. Eisenstadt confirms that constitutional decisional protection belongs to the individual even within marriage. These principles suffice without a new standalone equality, compelled-speech or general informational-secrecy holding. Section 3209 and dependent enforcement cannot stand. The notice and nonnotice-reason report would demand the same protected explanation and must also fall; separately lawful confidential data and the precise state-law severability consequences remain distinct. The Court preserves voluntary marital communication and decides no broader class of disclosure laws.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to State-enabled practical spousal control; the absence of a formal signature veto does not save this record's equivalent obstruction.
- Roe, 410 U.S. 113 — Its retained previability decision supplies the protected interest; no universal replacement scrutiny formula is adopted by this holding.
- Eisenstadt, 405 U.S. 438 — Applied for the individual's reproductive decision, including within marriage; no independent general secrecy rule is announced.
- Bellotti, 443 U.S. 622 — Distinguished as minor-parent cases involving separate parental responsibilities and adequate independent authorization; they do not confer comparable authority over a competent adult spouse.
- Hodgson, 497 U.S. 417 — Distinguished as minor-parent cases involving separate parental responsibilities and adequate independent authorization; they do not confer comparable authority over a competent adult spouse.
- Ohio v. Akron Center, 497 U.S. 502 — Distinguished as minor-parent cases involving separate parental responsibilities and adequate independent authorization; they do not confer comparable authority over a competent adult spouse.

### Notice-compliance reporting and the severable marital-status field

**Holding and operative rule.** The notice-given and nonnotice-reason demands in §3214(a)(12) are invalid as dependent enforcement of unconstitutional spousal notice. The distinct confidential marital-status question survives severance; the injunction must remove the notice-compliance fields and their enforcement while preserving that independently operating statistical question.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Notice-compliance reporting and the severable marital-status field,” joined at this scope by Blackmun, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter: 5 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Complete notice-field invalidation and independent marital-status severance; Stone additionally joins the notice-invalidity proposition only. Stone also joins invalidation of notice-given/nonnotice-reason reporting, making six for that proposition; he joins preservation of the independent marital-status field under the standing fallback without adding a ground. White, Scalia and Thomas sustain marital data on their primary intact-report premise and cast no conditional severance vote.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3214(a) bars patient names in these procedure reports. District finding 357, describing §3214(e)(1)–(2), states that the reports are not public records; law-enforcement disclosure requires a Court of Common Pleas order after an application showing good cause, and the Department’s statistical report must not disclose any physician, facility or patient’s identity. These procedure reports differ from the public facility reports. Source: supplied §3214(a) and district finding 357.  See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court separates the three questions within §3214(a)(12). A confidential report whether a patient is married does not itself require her to tell a spouse about the abortion. The further questions whether notice was given and why it was not serve the notice condition that the Court invalidates. Confidentiality does not make enforcement of that condition lawful. Danforth permits health records but does not permit reporting to accomplish an unconstitutional restriction. That limit requires removal of the notice-compliance questions and their dependent enforcement. The broad severability clause in Act 64, section 6, permits separation of provisions, words, phrases and applications when the remainder can operate independently. The marital-status question has an intelligible statistical operation without either notice question. Retaining it therefore severs an unlawful obligation without reconstructing the statute. The Court directs that distinction in the remand mandate. It does not invalidate all marital data or permit officials to demand the removed explanations through a differently labeled form.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Its prohibition on restrictive use of reporting is applied to invalid notice enforcement.
- Brockett v. Spokane Arcades, Inc., 472 U.S. 491 — Supports relief confined to the invalid obligation while preserving a legally separable remainder.
- The spousal-notice holding in this decision — Controls the notice-compliance dependency, not independently justified marital statistics.

### Confidential identification of referring physicians, agencies and services

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3214(a)(1)'s identification of a referring physician, agency or service, if any, survives this facial challenge as a limited confidential source record serving clinical follow-up and lawful compliance. This permission rests on the accepted confidentiality and the evidence concerning this field; it does not authorize patient identification, harassment, or a materially different restriction on referrals.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “Confidential identification of referring physicians, agencies and services,” joined at this scope by White, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 6 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Confidential clinical follow-up on the present record.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3214(a) bars patient names in these procedure reports. District finding 357, describing §3214(e)(1)–(2), states that the reports are not public records; law-enforcement disclosure requires a Court of Common Pleas order after an application showing good cause, and the Department’s statistical report must not disclose any physician, facility or patient’s identity. These procedure reports differ from the public facility reports. Source: supplied §3214(a) and district finding 357.  See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains the challenged referral field on the record before it. Danforth permits confidential reporting directed to maternal health and lawful medical administration, while forbidding recordkeeping used to accomplish an unconstitutional restriction. Referral sources can identify the path through which a patient obtained care and can have responsibilities connected to information and medical evaluation. Their identity therefore has a concrete administrative connection to the reported procedure. The district found that some physicians would stop referring even with adequate confidentiality. That evidence must be considered; the Court does not replace it with an assumption that confidentiality eliminates every burden. It nevertheless does not establish that this limited confidential identification requirement produces an unconstitutional restriction warranting facial relief. Thornburgh's objection to exposing patients does not resolve this different record, where individual-report confidentiality is accepted. The judgment leaves performing-physician identification and unappealed fields in their existing procedural position. It does not foreclose a demonstrated abusive use of information or a materially different application.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to confidential clinical reporting and its limit against accomplishing an unconstitutional restriction.
- Thornburgh, 476 U.S. 747 — Patient-exposure concerns are distinguished on the accepted confidentiality; its protection against restrictive reporting remains.

### Confidential basis for a medical-necessity judgment

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3214(a)(8) may require the physician's basis for finding an abortion necessary to prevent death or substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function when an abortion at 24 or more weeks’ gestational age was performed under §3211(b)(1). It requires documentation under that predicate, not advance approval, an additional clinical test, or a new holding on every postviability restriction.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Confidential basis for a medical-necessity judgment,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. After-care documentation of the precise necessity predicate.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3214(a) bars patient names in these procedure reports. District finding 357, describing §3214(e)(1)–(2), states that the reports are not public records; law-enforcement disclosure requires a Court of Common Pleas order after an application showing good cause, and the Department’s statistical report must not disclose any physician, facility or patient’s identity. These procedure reports differ from the public facility reports. Source: supplied §3214(a) and district finding 357.  See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court distinguishes a report of a physician's reasons from a requirement to obtain permission before treating a patient. For abortions at 24 or more weeks, section 3214(a)(8) concerns §3211(b)(1)’s necessity predicate: prevention of death or substantial and irreversible impairment of a major bodily function. It does not merely ask for gestational age or authorize officials to replace the governing medical judgment with their preferences. Danforth permits confidential information that contributes to medical knowledge and administration of lawful standards. The required explanation serves that purpose without creating a second medical decisionmaker. The district's concern about chilling professional judgment does not establish that this limited reporting duty itself imposes an unlawful medical veto. The reporting paragraph does not alter the governing medical-judgment standard or amend any applicable culpability requirement. The Court rejects this facial objection while leaving actual unlawful enforcement challengeable. It does not reopen the validity of unappealed provisions or decide a patient's entitlement under a different postviability rule.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to confidential health-related documentation.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — Existing law is not revised by this reporting permission; the common report-specific rationale does not newly adopt or reject Doe’s constitutional foundation.
- Thornburgh, 476 U.S. 747 — Its concern with burdens on medical judgment is applied to the actual limited duty, not treated as categorical invalidation of reasons reports.

### Confidential basis for an emergency judgment

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3214(a)(10) may require the basis for a medical emergency that excused compliance, after care and without making reporting a prerequisite to treatment. The predicate remains the physician's good-faith clinical judgment that immediate abortion is necessary to avert death or delay creates serious risk of substantial and irreversible impairment of major bodily function, under the protective Third Circuit construction accepted in this decision (§3203).

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Confidential basis for an emergency judgment,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. After-care documentation without narrowing the prospective emergency.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3214(a) bars patient names in these procedure reports. District finding 357, describing §3214(e)(1)–(2), states that the reports are not public records; law-enforcement disclosure requires a Court of Common Pleas order after an application showing good cause, and the Department’s statistical report must not disclose any physician, facility or patient’s identity. These procedure reports differ from the public facility reports. Source: supplied §3214(a) and district finding 357.  See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court sustains the emergency-basis report because it documents a judgment already made under the governing protective standard. Section 3203 concerns prospective risk assessed in good faith; it does not require a physician to wait until the threatened harm has occurred. Section 3214(a)(10) records why that emergency excused compliance with another requirement. Nothing in the reporting duty makes submission or administrative agreement a condition of prompt treatment. Danforth's permission for confidential health records supplies the relevant distinction between useful documentation and interference with care. The governing clinical-judgment standard remains intact, and the protective emergency construction prevents the report from silently narrowing the emergency itself. The concern that retrospective scrutiny will chill treatment does not establish an unlawful restriction from this bounded after-care report. Officials remain bound by the actual statutory predicates and applicable culpability requirements; the report grants no new power to punish a physician merely for a subsequently preferred medical choice. A concrete contrary enforcement practice may be challenged. No comprehensive immunity from malpractice, fraud or professional discipline is created.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to confidential documentation without a prior-permission condition.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — Existing law is not revised by this reporting permission; the common report-specific rationale does not newly adopt or reject Doe’s constitutional foundation.
- The medical-emergency holding in this decision — Its prospective good-faith construction governs the reported predicate; the report does not narrow it.

### Confidential account of gestational-age determination

**Holding and operative rule.** Section 3214(a)(11) may require the types of inquiries actually made, examinations and tests actually used, and the basis for the gestational-age diagnosis described in §3210(a). The underlying assessment uses the inquiries and procedures a prudent physician would consider necessary and is excused when an emergency prevents compliance; this report is distinct from §3214(a)(5)’s age result and imposes no fixed additional test.

**Authority.** Stevens's opinion, part “Confidential account of gestational-age determination,” joined at this scope by Stone, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 9 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Actual inquiry/test types and diagnostic basis; no new test or preapproval.

**Statutory terms and source.** Section 3214(a) bars patient names in these procedure reports. District finding 357, describing §3214(e)(1)–(2), states that the reports are not public records; law-enforcement disclosure requires a Court of Common Pleas order after an application showing good cause, and the Department’s statistical report must not disclose any physician, facility or patient’s identity. These procedure reports differ from the public facility reports. Source: supplied §3214(a) and district finding 357. District finding 309 quotes §3210(a): the performing physician reports the types of inquiries made, examinations and tests used, and the diagnostic basis; the underlying prudent-physician assessment is excused when a medical emergency prevents compliance. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Controlling explanation.** The Court considers the diagnostic-basis field under §3210(a), quoted in district finding 309. Section 3210(a) requires an assessment using inquiries, examinations and tests a prudent physician considers necessary, except when an emergency prevents compliance. The report records the types actually made or used and the diagnostic basis. The provision therefore differs from a compulsory test imposed regardless of medical usefulness, and from the separate field reporting the resulting age. Webster supports attention to the actual clinical obligation. Here, the reporting field imposes no fixed additional test; this permission adopts no comprehensive constitutional medical-usefulness test. Danforth permits limited confidential data serving health knowledge and lawful compliance. The claim that officials might misuse the report does not establish facial invalidity of this bounded documentation duty. The reporting duty leaves independently applicable clinical-judgment protections intact, and no advance approval follows. The judgment addresses the appealed reporting objection; the district-upheld underlying ascertainment requirement was not appealed and receives no new omnibus merits ruling here. Actual unlawful enforcement remains open to challenge.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Danforth, 428 U.S. 52 — Applied to limited confidential clinical data.
- Webster, 492 U.S. 490 — Applied to identify the actual clinical obligation; this reporting field imposes no fixed additional test, and no comprehensive constitutional usefulness test is adopted.
- Doe v. Bolton, 410 U.S. 179 — Existing law is not revised by this reporting permission; the common report-specific rationale does not newly adopt or reject Doe’s constitutional foundation.

### Public access to facility and affiliation reports

**Holding and operative rule.** The challenged public-release rule for facility and affiliation reports is sustained where the facility receives State-appropriated funds during the twelve-calendar-month period immediately before the request to inspect or copy the reports. Reports identify the facility and its parent, subsidiary or affiliated entities, including entities linked by contemporaneous ownership, beneficial interest, directorship or officership.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “Public access to facility and affiliation reports,” joined at this scope by White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 7 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Institutional public-funding accountability and the actual incremental-injury record.

**Statutory terms and source.** Reports of facilities not receiving State-appropriated funds remain available only to law-enforcement officials and the State Boards of Medicine and Osteopathic Medicine for official duties. The unchallenged duty to file and update reports remains separate. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** Statutory text: supplied appendix, section 3207(b). Its lookback runs from the inspection/copying request, not the report-filing date. Record findings and appellate treatment: full district reporting/public-release findings and the Third Circuit public-funding discussion distinguish actual harassment, feared incremental effects, funding for other services and the required constitutional nexus. Adequate confidentiality findings concerning patient-specific procedure reports do not convert these expressly public institutional reports into confidential records. The public permission rests on the institutional data and actual funding trigger; nonfunded section 3207(b) reports retain their specified official-use restriction. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that the Commonwealth may disclose this institutional information concerning a facility receiving public funds. The identities and affiliations of a publicly supported provider bear a reasonable relationship to accountability for the recipients and organization of public support, even when the funds assist services other than abortions. The public-release challenge is separate from the unchallenged obligation to provide the information to the department. The District Court found actual harassment, reasonable fears of increased harassment and likely refusal of funding that could impair services for indigent patients. Those findings are not dismissed. The record nevertheless does not establish a sufficiently supported incremental constitutional injury from this particular institutional disclosure to require facial invalidation. Thornburgh's concerns about intimidation do not make every disclosure of a funded facility's identity unlawful; its application must follow the actual information and evidence. No individual patient's identity becomes public through this report. The holding is limited to the statutory funding and request-date conditions and does not treat acceptance of public funds as a general surrender of constitutional rights.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Thornburgh — distinguishes the asserted intimidation concern according to this institutional information and record; no blanket authorization of disclosure targeting protected conduct follows.

### Public access to quarterly and trimester totals

**Holding and operative rule.** The challenged release of a facility's quarterly abortion total and trimester totals is sustained only if the facility receives State-appropriated funds within the twelve-calendar-month period immediately before filing the report. The form permits the facility to state whether it receives such funds.

**Authority.** O'Connor's opinion, part “Public access to quarterly and trimester totals,” joined at this scope by White, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas: 7 direct joins, an Opinion of the Court for the stated proposition. Aggregate public-funding accountability with the distinct filing lookback.

**Statutory terms and source.** If it states that it does not, the department must treat the report as confidential unless other evidence causes the department to conclude that the facility receives State-appropriated funds. This permission does not alter the unchallenged agency-collection requirement or authorize disclosure of an individual patient's identity. See [supplied statutory text](../briefs/OT_1991CHUNK14_CASEY_STATUTE.md); incorporated terms and source classifications are identified below.

**Record and construction.** Statutory text: supplied appendix, section 3214(f). The lookback runs from report filing, unlike section 3207(b)'s request-date lookback. Adequate security of confidential individual procedure reports is a district finding; those reports are not the same records as the expressly public aggregate totals. Using trimester groupings as statutory data categories does not itself decide a constitutional trimester test; the actual release conditions and no-funding/contrary-evidence rule govern this holding. Primary support: [district opinion, 744 F. Supp. 1323](https://static.case.law/f-supp/744/cases/1323-01.json); [Third Circuit opinion, 947 F.2d 682](https://static.case.law/f2d/947/cases/0682-01.json).

**Controlling explanation.** The Court holds that these aggregate reports may be made public under the provision's funding conditions. Quarterly procedure totals and their distribution across pregnancy stages reasonably inform the public about activity at an institution receiving State support. The statute does not make an individual patient's identity part of that public account. Confidential procedure-level reporting remains subject to its separate safeguards, which the District Court found adequate. The Court also accepts the findings of harassment and the risk that facilities may refuse funds, affecting care for poorer patients. Those concerns do not establish on this record the required connection between these particular aggregate disclosures and a constitutional injury warranting facial invalidation. Thornburgh's objection to intimidating disclosure remains a reason to examine the actual data and effects, not to assume every statistical report is impermissible. The holding preserves the filing-date lookback and the confidentiality of a no-funding report unless contrary evidence establishes State funding. It decides neither a general waiver of rights through funding nor the validity of every possible future disclosure practice.

**Precedent treatment.**

- Thornburgh — distinguishes this aggregate, funding-conditioned disclosure while preserving attention to actual intimidation and the information exposed.

## Precedent and current law

The entry-by-entry Precedent treatment paragraphs in Holdings are controlling at their stated scopes and are carried unchanged from the record. Roe’s constitutional foundation remains; its timetable changes under the separately joined timing holding. Akron’s contrary physician-delivery and fixed-delay applications are displaced for the requirements sustained here. Akron and Thornburgh do not categorically invalidate these concise accurate ancillary notices merely because they may favor childbirth; their distinct protections remain. No general replacement of Ohio v. Akron Center’s facial-review formulation is adopted. Marks does not supply a comprehensive formula or turn dissenting application votes into the controlling rationale of sustaining judgments. The superseded version 1 treatment supplies no additional current precedent rule.

Effective June 29, 1992, version 1.2 supplies Casey’s operative law. The version 1 record remains preserved but is superseded. Six retain protected choice and its viability/life-and-health boundaries; a different six remove the trimester timetable as an independent timing barrier. No complete replacement formula is adopted by five. The dedicated governing-standard holding states exactly how lower courts apply the direct provision holdings and surviving earlier authority. The new five-Justice delivery and waiting permissions rest on direct joins, not the superseded version 1 Marks aggregation. Accurate medical and concise ancillary notices have their stated independent permissions; spousal notice and notice-compliance reporting are invalid, with confidential marital-status data severed and retained. The narrow speech, parental and reporting rules have their own named coalitions. No independent emergency-vagueness, general large-fraction, equality, general marital-privacy or drug-admission holding is adopted. Published minority proposals remain noncontrolling.

### Issues not reached and exact separate treatment

| Independent issue | Justice-specific treatment | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Standalone emergency vagueness and its preservation | All nine leave an independent merits/waiver determination unmade; the emergency judgment rests on the adopted protective construction | The superseded version 1 clarity holding is not current law. No waiver is invented. |
| Distinct spousal First Amendment theory and its preservation | All nine leave independent merits and preservation unresolved; the six notice-invalidating Justices obtain complete relief on liberty. White, Scalia and Thomas would decide only their adjudicated grounds and leave ordinary handling of any genuinely preserved live theory | No speech holding or finding of abandonment follows from the notice judgment. |
| Independent spousal equal protection | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter do not reach an independently sufficient ground; O’Connor would invalidate on that ground; White, Scalia and Thomas would reject the claim, with different tier-selection positions stated in topology | Five do not reach; one would invalidate; three would sustain. No majority equality merits rule. |
| Independent marital/informational privacy | Stone, Blackmun, Stevens, Kennedy and Souter do not reach an independently sufficient general ground; O’Connor would invalidate on a narrow adult reproductive-communication ground; White, Scalia and Thomas reject the particular independent claim | Five do not reach; one would invalidate; three would sustain. Autonomy and coercion remain part of the controlling abortion-liberty rationale. |
| Provider speech concerning accurate medical content and concise ancillary notices | All nine reject the narrow clinical-content claim. Eight reject the narrow ancillary claim; Blackmun does not reach that additional ground because his liberty view supplies relief | No all-professional-speech commercial classification, universal factual-speech permission, or decision that every particular pamphlet is accurate. |
| General alternative-foundation remand if Roe is abandoned | The Court retains the constitutional foundation; that requested contingency does not occur | No general waiver or alternative constitutional foundation is adjudicated by inference. |
| Adult-incapacity guardian class; unappealed ordinary reports and underlying gestational ascertainment; unchallenged sex-selection and other postviability provisions; funding entitlement; fetal personhood; conscience sources | Outside fresh merits reach at this event | Existing procedural status and applicable independent law remain. No drug-admission entitlement or unpresented Benten claim is created. |

## Continuity and published separate writings

### Stone — Separate opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part

**Joins and scope.** Stevens joins the proposed substantive framework in Steps 1–5. The complete six-step replacement and focused remands have Stone alone. The separate application parts below have their expressly identified joins.

Chief Justice Stone joins the retained constitutional foundation and proposes replacing trimester machinery with a six-step undue-burden framework. Roe supplies protected choice, Doe clinical judgment, Danforth protection against transferred control, and Carey reproductive liberty. The proposal identifies the precise obligation and protected care; examines objectively established purpose; defines an affected population independently of the ultimate injury; measures causal and cumulative effects; requires justification and fit; and confines relief to unlawful obligations and applications. Poverty, geography, age, abuse and provider scarcity affect the burden without making every preexisting hardship State-caused.

A substantial obstacle to protected, medically appropriate previability care is independently forbidden. A smaller meaningful burden—concrete, nontrivial interference with access, health, privacy or informed decision-making—also fails for inadequate justification, excess relative to demonstrated benefit, or an unexplained practicable and substantially less burdensome adequate alternative. Challengers establish the obligation, group, causation and burden, or independently prove prohibited purpose; the State then substantiates benefit and fit. Reliable predictions may suffice before actual denial, without double counting or speculation. Opposition to abortion alone does not establish prohibited purpose, and lawful persuasion remains possible.

Necessary action against demonstrated medical danger remains permissible. Professional consensus has substantial evidentiary weight, not a veto; competent contrary evidence matters. Stone requires neither perfect policy, scientific certainty nor a new study for every rule. Ordinary proof and factual review remain, with independent constitutional review. Professionally defensible judgment is not punishable solely because of later disagreement, but malpractice, fraud and misconduct remain actionable. Before viability, clinical appropriateness does not require medical necessity or approval of the patient's reasons.

His sixth step permits facial relief for constitutionally prohibited terms or reliably demonstrated unlawful operation in a large fraction of an independently defined restricted group. It requires explanation of prevalence and inadequate narrower relief, supplies no numerical cutoff, and qualifies Ohio v. Akron Center’s no-valid-applications formulation. Stevens joins the substantive first five steps, without the generalized sixth-step rule. No complete framework receives five joins.

Stone would invalidate fixed waiting, unjustified physician exclusivity, automatic public facility releases and blanket referrer identification. He preserves accurate clinical information, concise accurate ancillary notices, limited confidential medical-basis reports and lawful parental involvement. Paternal-support information may be omitted in rape without police reporting (§3205(a)(2)(iii)); payment is not guaranteed. Materials are elective (§3205(a)(3)). He seeks focused materials and parental-attendance vacaturs and remands, with interim injunctions against disputed distribution and additional attendance; he does not invent a statutory visit command. Waiting invalidation would remain effective during remand. He joins spousal-notice invalidation and its dependent enforcement, while preserving voluntary consultation and independently lawful severable duties. His proposed remedies do not direct wholesale invalidation of the Act.

### Blackmun — Separate opinion concurring in part and dissenting in part

**Joins and scope.** No complete joins; agreement with a particular result elsewhere is not a join in this complete separate writing.

Justice Blackmun joins preservation of protected previability choice, the viability boundary, necessary life-and-health care, and the Court's reasons for retaining that constitutional foundation. He would also retain Roe's stronger staged scrutiny. His Webster opinion (1989) explains why changing medical facts warrant examination of particular regulations without abandoning protection of the decision.
